#5195. Chainalysis Describes Recovery Efforts After Mercado Bitcoin Security Incident

Strict editorial policy that focuses on accuracy, relevance, and impartiality

Morbi pretium leo et nisl aliquam mollis. Quisque arcu lorem, ultricies quis pellentesque nec, ullamcorper eu odio.

TL;DR: Chainalysis said on November 2 that it had supported tracing and containment efforts following a security incident involving Mercado Bitcoin. Incident estimates can differ between early reports, technical responders and the affected platform.

The difference between “identified”, “frozen” and “returned” is easy to lose in fast-moving security coverage. It matters to victims because only the last of those creates a completed recovery. Clear language also protects the credibility of investigators by avoiding a bigger success claim than the evidence supports.

Chainalysis said on November 2 that it had supported tracing and containment efforts following a security incident involving Mercado Bitcoin. The analytics company referred to an approximately $49 million incident and said nearly $10 million had been frozen with help from partners. These figures describe the company’s account of tracing and freezes, not a final audited loss statement or confirmed reimbursement to every customer. The dated original disclosure is the starting point for the figures and product scope discussed here.

Blockchain analytics can reconstruct movements between addresses and help investigators identify where suspected proceeds arrive. When funds reach a service with intervention powers, the operator may be able to freeze a balance or respond to a legal request. But tracing a token, freezing it and returning its value are three separate operations involving different evidence and legal approvals.

The response illustrates why cryptocurrency incidents increasingly depend on cooperation between issuers, investigators and exchanges. Decentralised settlement can be fast, while preservation of assets may require centralised intervention at a service endpoint. That tension is not new, but it becomes visible when investigators publish how much suspected value they could immobilise.

A crypto incident produces several separate timelines: containment, investigation, recovery and a return to reliable service. Those phases can overlap without finishing together. A traced wallet is not automatically frozen; a frozen balance is not automatically returned; and a reopened withdrawal route is not a completed security audit. Keeping those differences visible helps readers judge progress more accurately.

For additional perspective, it is worth reading our existing reporting on Binance’s earlier staged withdrawal restart and Bitcoin payment infrastructure and node software context. Those background reports help separate established facts from a new, still-developing announcement.

Incident estimates can differ between early reports, technical responders and the affected platform. Figures should therefore be attributed and not blended into a supposedly exact final loss. A freeze does not establish the funds can be released immediately, and analysts should not equate linked addresses with a legal finding against every downstream wallet holder.

Operational controls matter most when a business is under stress. Security announcements should identify the system affected, the customers who can act, the evidence supporting any recovery number and what remains uncertain. Clear scope protects users from false reassurance and from unnecessary panic. It also provides a sensible basis for follow-up accountability.

Follow official updates for verified loss totals, recovery procedures and whether the affected platform changes its custody or operational controls. For users, the practical questions are whether balances remain accessible, how claims will be handled and what evidence supports any reimbursement promise. A transparent accounting of outcomes is more important than a dramatic seizure headline.

#5194. Ethereum’s decentralization model is the answer to an AI arms race: Vitalik Buterin

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

Is growing AI risk setting the stage for wider blockchain adoption? Vitalik Buterin certainly thinks so.

Speaking at Devcon 8 in Mumbai, India, Ethereum’s co-founder said that centralizing AI tools could lead to an AI arms race. So how can this be an opportunity for blockchain?

Structurally, blockchains operate through decentralized networks, reducing dependence on a single entity. But centralized networks suffer from single points of failure, meaning that an attack could take down the whole network.

Decentralized networks such as Ethereum reduce this risk by spreading control across multiple participants. Naturally, the question becomes: Could growing AI-driven risks strengthen Ethereum’s long-term investment case?

Interestingly, on-chain data is already backing this setup.

According to ValidatorQueue, the number of ETH staked on the Ethereum blockchain has reached a new all-time high of 44.3 million, which now accounts for nearly 36.3% of the total supply.

By locking up ETH, users are proving their commitment to securing the network, further strengthening its relevance as demand for decentralized infrastructure grows amid rising AI-driven risks.

That said, Ethereum [ETH] is not the only decentralized network. The bigger question is whether its established ecosystem and network security give ETH an edge in capturing this growing demand.

Ethereum’s network security brings its long-term investment potential into focus.

The logic is simple: Vitalik Buterin has raised the concerns about the dangers of centralized AI and introduced the concept of Defensive Decentralized Acceleration, which is based on open-source software, trustworthy hardware, and cryptography.

This could open a new growth avenue for Ethereum, making decentralized infrastructure a stronger consideration for long-term investors building their crypto portfolios.

Notably, the timing is interesting. A recent analysis using the SEC’s crypto guidance found that Ethereum was the most decentralized (9.7), followed by Solana (8.8) and XRP (8.1).

This plays into the narrative around Ethereum’s decentralization. As demand grows for secure infrastructure, on-chain data is already showing Ethereum as the most decentralized option.

In this context, Vitalik Buterin’s concerns around centralized AI could be more than just a warning.

Instead, it highlights how the market may be moving towards a more decentralized infrastructure and will play to Ethereum’s advantage to capitalize on increased demand for open networks.

So, while this does not guarantee ETH price appreciation, it adds another long-term adoption narrative to its investment case.

Growing AI risks could increase demand for decentralized networks like Ethereum.

Rising ETH staking and Ethereum’s strong security could support its long-term investment case.

#5192. Why rising XRP whale counts are a fake bullish signal

A split of XRP stolen from Binance created six large accounts, showing how wallet counts can grow through transfers without establishing fresh buying.

A redistribution of XRP stolen from Binance created six new accounts above the one-million-token threshold on Nov. 2, showing how large-wallet counts can grow through transfers alone.

Blockchain analytics firm Bitquery traced the split from one receiving account into seven new accounts, with six clearing the million-token threshold.

An address tally can increase through the movement of existing holdings, making its interpretation important before drawing a bullish conclusion.

Binance's Nov. 3 report said five of its hot wallets sent 233.46 million XRP to an attacker-controlled address between 20:05 and 20:09 UTC on Oct. 29.

Bitquery's address list shows that five Nov. 2 recipients received 40,001,000 XRP each. Another received 33,455,920 XRP, alongside the small seventh account.

For a raw count of addresses holding at least one million XRP, six qualifying recipients replacing one qualifying sender would mean a net increase of five. That calculation requires the sender to have been counted beforehand, all six recipients to qualify afterward, and consistent address coverage and observation cutoffs.

Amounts received on Nov. 2, 2026; account addresses are abbreviated. Six recipients exceeded one million XRP. The seventh received 1,000 XRP, too small to see clearly on this linear scale; the exact value remains in the table. These transfers do not establish separate owners or new buying.

Source: Bitquery investigation: XRP recipient list (Nov. 2, 2026)

A single pool of stolen XRP can appear as several large balances, without requiring another buyer. Distinguishing redistribution from accumulation involves tracing the tokens' source and identifying which addresses belong together.

Binance said in its Nov. 3 update that it would absorb the attack's financial impact and that 98% of impacted token assets had been replenished as of Oct. 31.

For investors assessing XRP's recovery, the traced split puts the origin of large balances ahead of their number.

Rising wallet counts become more informative when they connect to new buying and independently held balances. Here, a transfer of existing stolen tokens is sufficient to explain the creation of six large accounts.

#5191. How to Swap Bitcoin to Ethereum Without a Bridge

Home » Cryptopedia » How to Swap Bitcoin to Ethereum Without a Bridge

The process, requirements, fees, and risks involved in swapping Bitcoin for Ethereum without using a bridge.

You can swap Bitcoin to Ethereum without a bridge by using a native cross-chain swap protocol such as THORChain. The process takes six steps: open a compatible swap interface, connect your Bitcoin wallet, enter the amount and your Ethereum receiving address, review the quote, sign the transaction, and wait for native ETH to arrive in your own wallet. No wrapped token is created, no account is needed and no custodian holds your funds. You send real BTC on the Bitcoin network and receive real ETH on the Ethereum network.

A bridge moves value between chains by locking your BTC and issuing a wrapped version on Ethereum. That wrapped token is a claim on BTC held by a custodian or bridge contract, so its value depends on that backing staying secure for as long as you hold it.

A native swap skips the wrapping. The protocol holds liquidity on each connected chain and pays out the real asset you ask for. Once the swap settles, you hold genuine ETH in your own wallet with no outstanding claim on anyone.

You do not need ETH in advance. The Ethereum network fee for delivering your ETH is deducted from the swap output.

The protocol is accessed through an interface. The official web interface is THORChain Swap, and the protocol is also integrated into a range of wallets and swap apps, including Vultisig and Keystore, with hardware wallets such as Ledger supported through compatible interfaces. Every interface routes to the same underlying liquidity pools.

Type the interface address directly or use a saved bookmark rather than following links from ads or messages, as copycat swap sites are a common phishing method.

Connect the wallet that holds your BTC. Connecting lets the interface read your balance and prepare the transaction, but it does not give the interface control of your funds. You approve every transaction yourself, and your private keys never leave your wallet.

The official interface also offers a walletless option, where you send BTC to a deposit address generated for your swap instead of connecting a wallet.

Select BTC as the asset to sell and ETH on Ethereum as the asset to buy, then enter the amount. Paste the Ethereum address where you want to receive the ETH. This must be an address you control on the Ethereum network.

The interface shows the expected ETH output and the costs involved. These typically include the Bitcoin network fee for sending your BTC, a liquidity fee that rises with trade size relative to pool depth, and an outbound fee that covers the Ethereum transaction delivering your ETH.

Behind the scenes, a BTC to ETH swap passes through two pools, BTC to RUNE and then RUNE to ETH, with RUNE acting as the settlement asset. The quote already accounts for both legs. Swaps are ordered by price impact within each block, which is designed to resist sandwich attacks and other front-running.

For large amounts, check whether the interface offers streaming swaps, which split a trade into smaller pieces across multiple blocks to reduce price impact.

When you confirm, your wallet asks you to sign a standard Bitcoin transaction. It sends your BTC to the network’s inbound vault with a short memo attached, which tells the network what to swap into and where to send it. The interface writes the memo for you.

These vaults are controlled jointly by the network’s validators, called THORNodes, using threshold signatures, so no single operator holds the keys. Each THORNode bonds RUNE as collateral and can be slashed for malicious behavior.

The network’s validators observe your BTC deposit once it has enough Bitcoin confirmations, execute the swap and send native ETH from the network’s Ethereum vault to your address. Total time depends mainly on how quickly the Bitcoin network confirms your transaction. Most interfaces include a tracker that shows each stage of the swap.

The swap is complete when native ETH appears at your Ethereum address. You can confirm it in your wallet or by looking up your address on an Ethereum block explorer. Compare the amount received against the quoted output; small differences come from price movement between quoting and execution.

If a swap cannot be completed, for example because the price moved beyond your slippage limit, the protocol refunds the BTC to your sending address, minus network fees.

Native swaps remove the depeg risk and ongoing custody risk that come with holding a wrapped token. They do not remove every risk. While a swap is in progress, funds pass through the protocol’s vaults, so you rely on the protocol’s code, its validator set and its economic security model during that window. As with all DeFi, there is smart contract and protocol risk. Start with a small test swap if you are new to the process.

Yes. Native swap protocols such as THORChain exchange real BTC on the Bitcoin network for real ETH on the Ethereum network. No wrapped bitcoin is created at any point. You send BTC from your own wallet and receive ETH in your own wallet, so there is no wrapped token left to hold or trust after the swap.

No account is required. THORChain is a non-custodial protocol, so you swap directly from a wallet you control rather than depositing funds with a platform. Some third-party interfaces built on the protocol may apply their own policies, so check the terms of the interface you use.

Timing depends mostly on the Bitcoin network. The network waits for enough Bitcoin confirmations before processing the swap, and larger deposits can require more confirmations. Once the deposit is confirmed, the swap and the outbound ETH transaction are processed by the network. Interfaces usually show an estimated time with the quote.

If a swap cannot complete, for example because the price moved past your slippage limit, THORChain refunds your BTC to the address it came from, minus network fees. Using a fresh quote and letting the interface write the memo are the best ways to avoid failed swaps.

No. You pay the Bitcoin network fee when sending BTC, and the cost of the Ethereum transaction that delivers your ETH is deducted from the swap output as an outbound fee. This means you can receive ETH into a brand-new Ethereum address with a zero balance.

#5190. Chainalysis Discloses Separate October 30 Security Incident Involving User Data

Strict editorial policy that focuses on accuracy, relevance, and impartiality

Morbi pretium leo et nisl aliquam mollis. Quisque arcu lorem, ultricies quis pellentesque nec, ullamcorper eu odio.

TL;DR: Chainalysis disclosed a security incident on October 30 affecting a subset of customer-related information, including names, email addresses and certain user or role labels, according to the company. “No evidence of compromise” is not proof that a particular system was mathematically impossible to access.

The apparent boundary between identity information and investigative data is the central fact in this disclosure. Readers should not dismiss exposed contact details as harmless, but neither should they infer a breach of every product the company operates. Precision is particularly important when the firm itself works in cybercrime investigations.

Chainalysis disclosed a security incident on October 30 affecting a subset of customer-related information, including names, email addresses and certain user or role labels, according to the company. It said there was no evidence that its investigative graph data, credentials or user sessions were compromised. This was a separate incident and should not be merged with Chainalysis’ analysis of the Binance theft. The dated original disclosure is the starting point for the figures and product scope discussed here.

Businesses that help investigate blockchain crime may also hold conventional personal and organisational information about their customers. Even when a breach does not expose transaction intelligence, names and roles can be useful to attackers crafting phishing or social-engineering messages. That risk depends on the exact data exposed and how those records can be combined with information already public.

The disclosure underscores the value of keeping customer identity systems, investigation data and access credentials in well-defined security boundaries. A malicious actor with one type of information should not automatically gain another. The company’s description can be used to understand the stated scope, but good incident response also includes validating those claims over time.

A crypto incident produces several separate timelines: containment, investigation, recovery and a return to reliable service. Those phases can overlap without finishing together. A traced wallet is not automatically frozen; a frozen balance is not automatically returned; and a reopened withdrawal route is not a completed security audit. Keeping those differences visible helps readers judge progress more accurately.

This development also connects to two subjects our newsroom has been following: Binance’s earlier staged withdrawal restart and Bitcoin payment infrastructure and node software context. Those background reports help separate established facts from a new, still-developing announcement.

“No evidence of compromise” is not proof that a particular system was mathematically impossible to access. It is a statement about what investigators had found as of the company’s update. Affected customers should rely on verified notices and avoid clicking unsolicited reset links. Equally, the existence of a data event does not prove that all users’ credentials were stolen.

Operational controls matter most when a business is under stress. Security announcements should identify the system affected, the customers who can act, the evidence supporting any recovery number and what remains uncertain. Clear scope protects users from false reassurance and from unnecessary panic. It also provides a sensible basis for follow-up accountability.

Next, look for the company’s follow-up findings, notice procedures and any confirmed misuse of exposed information. For organisations using investigative vendors, the broader lesson is that sensitive workflows should limit data sharing and use strong independent authentication. Vendor risk exists even when the core analytical product is technically sound.

#5187. Polkadot lost $1.38 million after hacker exploited security flaw fixed four months earlier

The attacker exploited a vulnerability fixed in June but missing from production, targeting Polkadot's newly launched dotUSD stablecoin and exposing weaknesses in its security response.

Polkadot's Treasury lost $1.38 million after an attacker exploited a vulnerability developers had fixed months before its new stablecoin launched.

In a Nov. 2 incident report, Parity Technologies, a blockchain infrastructure firm focused on the Polkadot and Kusama ecosystems, revealed that the security flaw had been identified and corrected in its codebase in June but never reached the affected production systems before the Oct. 24-26 attack.

The disclosure exposes a security breakdown surrounding dotUSD, Polkadot's decentralized stablecoin, which launched in October under the governance of the network's decentralized autonomous organization (DAO).

Unlike stablecoins issued by private companies, dotUSD was designed to operate without a centralized issuer, with DOT holders governing decisions through the network's OpenGov system.

However, the vulnerability predated the stablecoin's introduction. Parity initially classified the bug as medium severity because its assessment did not account for how the flaw could interact with dotUSD, which had not yet launched.

The developer later upgraded the vulnerability to critical after the attack exposed its financial consequences.

The breach also followed a public disclosure on Oct. 20, when the security researcher who originally reported the vulnerability posted a forum comment referencing the code fix.

Parity said the researcher removed the comment after being notified that it violated the company's bug bounty rules. The developer believes the disclosure may have given attackers enough information to identify and exploit the vulnerability.

The attacker ultimately manipulated Polkadot Asset Hub's cross-chain messaging system to reduce dotUSD's recorded supply without removing tokens from users' accounts.

That discrepancy distorted prices in the network's DOT/dotUSD liquidity pool, allowing traders to exchange dotUSD for DOT at artificial rates.

The Treasury, which was the pool's sole liquidity provider, absorbed approximately $1.381 million in losses.

Parity stressed that no users lost funds and dotUSD remained fully backed by USDT. However, the company could not establish whether the person who exploited the vulnerability also profited from the subsequent arbitrage trades.

Parity merged a fix for the XCM bug in June, but it did not reach the affected runtimes before the October dotUSD exploit. The containment halt could be bypassed.

Snowfork stopped its own relayers. The XCM flaw remained, and other operators could still run relayers.

The attacker ran its own consensus relayer and resumed about six hours before the XCM fix took effect.

Snowfork stopped its own relayers, but the attacker ran another. Runtime 2.5.1 fixed XCM on Oct. 26; 2.5.2 reconciled dotUSD supply on Oct. 30.

Parity said the Oct. 20 public comment may have helped attackers; that link remains unproven. The identity of the arbitrage beneficiary is unresolved. The Treasury was the pool’s sole liquidity provider, and users lost no funds. The timeline is not a record of bridge availability.

Timeline follows Parity’s Nov. 2 incident review and executed referenda 1960 and 1961; the permissionless relay explanation uses Snowbridge documentation. Dates mark the reported sequence; spacing does not encode duration. Loss is Parity’s estimate.

Parity said the Oct. 20 public comment may have helped attackers; that link remains unproven. The identity of the arbitrage beneficiary is unresolved. The Treasury was the pool’s sole liquidity provider, and users lost no funds. The timeline is not a record of bridge availability.

The incident became more complicated when the attacker circumvented an emergency containment measure intended to stop the exploit.

After suspicious activity was detected on Oct. 24, Snowfork halted its consensus relayers, temporarily preventing further malicious transactions from reaching Polkadot through the Snowbridge network.

However, the attacker operated an independent relayer on Oct. 26, resuming the exploit approximately six hours before developers deployed the security upgrade through OpenGov.

The workaround was possible because Snowbridge allows independent participants to operate relayers, meaning Snowfork's intervention could interrupt its own infrastructure without permanently blocking the attacker's access.

Parity eventually closed the vulnerability through runtime upgrade 2.5.1 on Oct. 26 and rescued the remaining liquidity from the affected trading pool.

A second upgrade followed on Oct. 30, correcting dotUSD's recorded supply without changing individual account balances and resolving a separate issue that had disrupted Snowbridge.

The recovered dotUSD was returned to the Treasury, although the financial losses from the artificial trading activity remained.

Parity acknowledged that its existing security processes failed to account for the evolving risk introduced by new network functionality.

The company said it would strengthen vulnerability assessments, improve deployment checks and expand monitoring for unusual asset issuance.

It is also refining an automated AI-based security framework to identify potentially exploitable code changes earlier in development, while reassessing previously reported vulnerabilities for risks introduced by dotUSD.

Oluwapelumi values Bitcoin's potential. He imparts insights on a range of topics like DeFi, hacks, mining and culture, underlining transformative power.

#5185. 'Don’t Be Silly': Satoshi Associate Dismisses Bitcoin Quantum Collapse Claims

Disclaimer: The opinions expressed by our writers are their own and do not represent the views of U.Today. The financial and market information provided on U.Today is intended for informational purposes only. U.Today is not liable for any financial losses incurred while trading cryptocurrencies. Conduct your own research by contacting financial experts before making any investment decisions. We believe that all content is accurate as of the date of publication, but certain offers mentioned may no longer be available.

Bitcoin developers have renewed the discussion about the long-term security of the network amid the development of quantum computing ("Q-Day"). The trigger was an interview StarkWare CEO Eli Ben-Sasson gave to journalist Pete Rizzo.

According to Ben-Sasson, without the implementation of a "quantum fix," Bitcoin will lose its leadership, and its market value will collapse due to the physical limitations of current computing systems.

Criticism of these forecasts came from Adam Back, the creator of Hashcash, co-founder of Blockstream and one of the cryptographers mentioned by Satoshi Nakamoto in the Bitcoin whitepaper. On the social network X, Back described the panic expectations with the phrase: "c'mon guys, don't be silly and alarmist."

According to Back, the risks are exaggerated, since algorithms for protection against quantum threats are already at the protocol planning stage. He pointed out that Ben-Sasson's own company, StarkWare, builds its products on STARK technology: zero-knowledge proofs that run on cryptographic hash functions.

In the Bitcoin technical community, hash functions are considered the main security factor. According to theoretical models, quantum computers running Shor's algorithm are capable of breaking the network's current asymmetric cryptography (ECDSA and Schnorr).

However, this method is ineffective against hash functions (including SHA-256). Grover's algorithm gives quantum systems only a quadratic speedup in brute-force search, which, according to Back, is fully neutralized by Bitcoin's standard security settings.

As an alternative scenario, Satoshi Nakamoto's associate cited data on the community's current technical developments, which by late 2026 have moved into the testing phase:

SHRINCS protocol: A modified hash-based signature (Shrunken SPHINCS). According to Blockstream developers, they optimized it for Bitcoin's bandwidth limits.

Implementation status: An official draft Bitcoin Improvement Proposal (BIP) has been published for review. According to the project's roadmap, the technology is undergoing stress tests on the Liquid sidechain.

Critics of post-quantum cryptography often name data volume as the main problem. Quantum-resistant signatures are bulkier than standard 64-byte Schnorr keys, which, according to a number of researchers, could overload the blockchain and raise fees.

Back argues that the infrastructure is capable of scaling through data aggregation. According to his position, batch verification methods and STARK hash proofs make it possible to compress thousands of signatures into one compact package. According to supporters of this approach, the solution will preserve the network's speed and protect its decentralization.

The current discussion demonstrates the existence of two polar views: part of the industry considers the quantum threat an urgent problem, while Bitcoin core developers speak of planned testing of the protective infrastructure, which is proceeding in parallel with the development of quantum capabilities.

#5184. ECB Links Europe’s T+1 Securities Transition to a Broader Settlement Overhaul

Strict editorial policy that focuses on accuracy, relevance, and impartiality

Morbi pretium leo et nisl aliquam mollis. Quisque arcu lorem, ultricies quis pellentesque nec, ullamcorper eu odio.

TL;DR: European Central Bank officials have discussed the planned move to T+1 securities settlement in October 2027 alongside longer-term work on tokenized settlement infrastructure. A future regulatory settlement date should not be confused with a finished network rollout.

The deadline matters most to firms that process transactions across several jurisdictions. They have to harmonise instructions, reconcile holdings and manage currencies while the clock gets shorter. Blockchain advocates should explain specifically which tasks become easier and which stay with regulated intermediaries.

European Central Bank officials have discussed the planned move to T+1 securities settlement in October 2027 alongside longer-term work on tokenized settlement infrastructure. A speech published October 29 outlined how shortening the conventional settlement cycle and experimenting with new technology fit into wider financial-market modernisation. The two projects are related strategic topics, not a statement that blockchain technology will automatically replace Europe’s existing settlement systems next year. The dated original disclosure is the starting point for the figures and product scope discussed here.

T+1 means most eligible securities trades settle one business day after the trade date, compared with a longer conventional cycle. Shorter cycles can reduce outstanding exposures and certain capital needs, but they compress the time available to resolve breaks, fund positions and coordinate across time zones. Tokenization addresses a different design question: how ownership records and money can move using new technical infrastructure.

The connection is operational discipline. A market trying to settle sooner needs robust automation, accurate records and predictable funding. Tokenized assets may eventually support more tightly connected workflows, but they are not necessary to achieve the near-term T+1 timetable. Existing post-trade institutions and banks still have crucial responsibilities.

A tokenized instrument must connect technical records with enforceable financial rights. Trading software can move a token quickly, but an issuer, custodian, transfer agent or settlement bank may still have to recognise the result. This is why tokenization announcements need to explain which part of the lifecycle is changing, not merely advertise that a blockchain is involved.

This development also connects to two subjects our newsroom has been following: DTCC’s tokenized securities infrastructure rollout and tokenized fund redemptions tested over a weekend. They explain related market structure and should not be mistaken for evidence that every new proposal is already live.

A future regulatory settlement date should not be confused with a finished network rollout. Different products and markets can have exclusions or distinct implementation rules. Nor should a policy speech be read as authorising every private tokenized security to circulate without existing investor-protection requirements.

Real settlement infrastructure is judged by whether it works on bad days, not just during presentations. Reconciliation, asset eligibility, funding, corporate actions and recovery from errors remain central. Continuous technical operation can improve coordination, yet it does not create market liquidity or remove the obligations of regulated participants.

Watch for detailed industry testing, bank funding arrangements and official updates on both T+1 readiness and central-bank-money experiments. The important measure is whether settlement failures and costs fall without shifting uncontrolled risk to participants. Europe’s timeline creates a concrete deadline, while the tokenization debate remains a longer-term architecture choice.

Ethereum’s recovery is an indication of a temporary halt to heavy selling activity in October and not a solid indication of renewed purchasing interest.

Subsequently, buying pressure lifted the price to $2,339.36, up by 2.28%. Nonetheless, the overall trend in ETH, as shown by the four-hour chart, continues to be comprised of lower highs and lower lows.

Therefore, this simply implies sellers are clearly still in charge.

Now the recovery faces potential resistance at the 50% Fibonacci level at $2,360.70. A successful breach of this level could propel ETH towards $2,403.95, where sellers will likely protect the larger downtrend.

Scores ranging from 25 to 49 represent an uncertainty where the selling pressure may decrease but does not trigger an immediate surge in buying demand.

#5182. XRP’s 500+ Accounts Grew 14% Through a Steep Price Slide

Home » Trendsetter News » XRP’s 500+ Accounts Grew 14% Through a Steep Price Slide

XRP Ledger accounts holding 500 tokens & more kept rising during a 64% price dip, according to snapshots cited by Cheeky Crypto.

A 64% XRP price decline coincided with a 14% increase in accounts holding at least 500 tokens, according to ledger snapshots cited by Cheeky Crypto in a fresh video. The apparent contradiction anchors the host’s bullish argument, but the figures measure accounts, not individual investors or their conviction.

The analyst reports that accounts above the threshold increased from roughly 1.26 million to 1.44 million over the period examined. At the video’s reference price of about $1.25, a 500-XRP holding was worth approximately $627, against roughly $1,775 at the cited record closing price.

About one in six XRP Ledger accounts holds at least 500 XRP, the analyst says. Excluding accounts with fewer than 20 tokens puts that proportion closer to one in three.

Those near-empty accounts matter: approximately 4.3 million of the ledger’s more than 8 million accounts held under 20 XRP in the cited dataset. Their presence makes a modest balance appear more exceptional in the overall ranking.

Exchange custody complicates the picture further. A single exchange address can represent many customers, while one investor can control several addresses.

Someone holding 500 XRP on an exchange therefore may not appear as a separate account in the distribution.

“These are just net counts,” the analyst acknowledges. Growth cannot distinguish new purchases from transfers, account splitting or other changes, and does not establish that existing holders refused to sell.

Cheeky Crypto’s own outlook draws partly on historical days when XRP closed 60% to 70% below its running high.

In that analysis, prices were higher two years later in 79% of observations, but the overlapping dates represented only a handful of distinct market episodes, not hundreds of independent tests.

The shorter-term record was weaker: the median one-year outcome was approximately 9% lower. Illustrative scenarios put 500 XRP at about $445 with a token price of $0.89, $1,200 at $2.40 and $2,000 at $4. Cheeky Crypto explicitly presents these as scenarios, not forecasts.

Surely, the account growth is evidence of a changing ledger distribution, not proof of stronger demand. Custody arrangements and concentrated holdings limit what this measure alone can reveal about XRP’s next price move.

Evernorth’s route to additional XRP buying cleared a registration condition on Oct. 30, but weaker shares have reduced the value of its original Cantor allowance to about $63 million in a closing-price illustration, before discounts and expenses.

Those figures measure the same original allotment at two dated closing prices.

Evernorth’s Oct. 26 announcement described approximately $80 million of gross proceeds if the facility were fully executed at the Oct. 22 closing price. The commitment is denominated in shares, so a lower price means less cash from the same number of shares.

XRPN closed at $14.20 on Oct. 30, fell to $11.70 on Nov. 2 and recovered to $11.98 on Nov. 3. The latest completed session therefore left the stock below the reference price behind the earlier financing illustration.

The $62.5 million illustration at the Nov. 3 close equates to roughly 48.8 million XRP at a constant $1.28 per token. The $80.7 million illustration at the Oct. 22 close equates to 63.0 million XRP at that same token price. Two dated XRPN closing prices put different cash values on the same original 5,218,935-share allowance.

Under the purchase agreement, Cantor ordinarily pays 97% of volume-weighted average price during the applicable purchase period. That is a 3% discount to the period’s average, rather than a promise to pay the closing price used in the illustration.

Purchases are also generally constrained by 20% of trading volume during that period, the company’s notice estimate and a 4.99% beneficial-ownership limit. Special terms apply to accepted requests above the ordinary participation percentage.

Evernorth also disclosed fee obligations: a nonrefundable $521,893.50 upfront commitment fee, a possible upward adjustment capped at $1 million in total commitment fees, expenses and a separate $200,000 qualified-independent-underwriter fee. The commitment-fee cap does not cover all costs.

Effectiveness satisfies one commencement condition. Other contractual requirements include a final prospectus and compliance, legal, listing and transfer-agent deliverables. Management decides whether and when to send purchase notices.

Fresh XRP demand from this facility requires company share issuance, settled cash proceeds and subsequent XRP acquisition. Cantor’s later resales are a separate transaction: those receipts belong to Cantor. Ordinary secondary stock trading does not itself put cash in Evernorth’s treasury.

As of Nov. 4, the available disclosures did not establish actual facility shares sold, settled proceeds, remaining capacity or subsequent XRP acquisitions. The SEC filing record and company announcements leave those amounts unresolved, without establishing that the facility has gone unused.

The decisive next signal would be a dated company disclosure connecting share-sale receipts to XRP acquired. That would turn potential buying power into documented token demand.

A new blockchain project, Yellow, that previously attracted $10 million in personal investment from Ripple co-founder Chris Larsen has faced massive criticism from members of the XRP community immediately after the listing of its token. Observers’ attention was drawn to asset-selling operations from a wallet that is linked to the developers.

#5179. SEC Updates Crypto-Asset FAQs as Firms Seek Clarity on Federal Securities Rules

Strict editorial policy that focuses on accuracy, relevance, and impartiality

Morbi pretium leo et nisl aliquam mollis. Quisque arcu lorem, ultricies quis pellentesque nec, ullamcorper eu odio.

TL;DR: The SEC Division of Corporation Finance added questions 1.4, 1.5 and 1.6 to its crypto-assets FAQ on November 2, 2026. SEC staff positions may be informative without having the legal force of a statute, judicial judgment or final Commission rule.

This distinction is worth making because crypto regulation often arrives in several formats: speeches, staff statements, enforcement actions, proposals and final rules. They have different implications even when the subject is the same. A dated FAQ update merits attention without being promoted into a sweeping legal turning point.

The SEC Division of Corporation Finance added questions 1.4, 1.5 and 1.6 to its crypto-assets FAQ on November 2, 2026. The new staff answers address governance through foundations or organisations, what counts as control over participation and the difference between a functional network and a decentralised one. The page expressly says its answers represent staff views, have no legal force and have not been approved or disapproved by the Commission. The dated original disclosure is the starting point for the figures and product scope discussed here.

A functioning crypto system can have a native asset that users need for programmatic activity without necessarily being decentralised. Under the staff explanation, decentralisation is a distinct question about whether any party or group holds operational, economic or voting control. A foundation or association involved in broad-based governance is not automatically a controlling central party; the actual powers and decision rules matter. This is a narrower, more useful clarification than saying every governance token has received a new legal classification.

The additions give lawyers and builders more concrete language for evaluating governance arrangements. A protocol may distribute votes widely while retaining privileged access, unilateral upgrade powers or concentrated economic influence. The staff view asks analysts to study those powers rather than rely on the mere existence of a formal foundation. That could influence how firms document their networks, although it does not eliminate case-by-case analysis or statutory legal requirements.

Crypto policy updates arrive as proposals, staff explanations, enforcement cases and final rules. Those documents carry different legal weight even when they use similar language. A careful article tells readers who issued the text, when it was published and whether it changes obligations now. That distinction is especially important for financial firms making compliance decisions.

This development also connects to two subjects our newsroom has been following: DTCC’s tokenization service and settlement controls and financial infrastructure and technology policy coverage. Those background reports help separate established facts from a new, still-developing announcement.

SEC staff positions may be informative without having the legal force of a statute, judicial judgment or final Commission rule. Investors should also resist the leap from regulatory discussion to token-price predictions. A frequently asked questions page is not an approval notice for any specific exchange, security or stablecoin.

Regulation ultimately affects operational choices: where customer assets are held, who can move them, how decisions are recorded and which risks are disclosed. The market can debate policy direction, but firms still need to map any published guidance to existing duties and the specific activity they carry out. General labels rarely answer those questions.

Watch for direct Commission statements, formal rulemaking notices, court decisions and clarifications that identify the affected transactions. Teams reviewing compliance can use the FAQ alongside primary legal sources, while documenting areas where further advice is necessary. Precision about who issued a document is essential to accurate policy reporting.

Reece Merrick, Ripple Senior Executive Officer/Managing Director, Middle East & Africa, attended the recently concluded Dubai Fintech Summit held between November 2 and 3, 2026, organized by DIFC as part of Dubai Future Finance Week.

In 2024, Ripple announced a partnership with the DIFC Innovation Hub, the thriving innovation ecosystem of the Dubai International Financial Centre (DIFC), to accelerate blockchain innovation in the UAE.

Ripple announced in 2026 its expansion in the UAE with the opening of its Middle East and Africa (MEA) regional headquarters in the Dubai International Financial Centre (DIFC). The announcement highlighted the firm's strategic presence in the country.

Fantastic panel at @DubaiFinTechSum diving into how digital assets scale from here. The consensus was clear: the question is no longer if institutional adoption happens, but what infrastructure needs to change to support it. A few takeaways from our discussion with @BlackRock, @StanChart & @RobinhoodCrypto ➡️ The 24/7 reality: The next generation requires always-on rails. Markets with "opening hours" simply won't cut it. ➡️ Bridging TradFi & DeFi: Scaling tokenized markets requires real bank-grade infrastructure, custody, and risk management. At @Ripple, we see every day how enterprise rails, tokenization, and stablecoins bridge this gap 🇦🇪

In a recent X post, Merrick highlighted his participation at the Dubai Fintech Summit as part of a panel that explored how digital assets could scale. The details gathered from his X post revealed that representatives from BlackRock, Standard Chartered and Robinhood were also participants in this discussion.

Merrick said the consensus among participants was clear, as the question was no longer whether institutional adoption would happen, but what infrastructure needed to change to support it.

The Ripple executive went on to share a few takeaways from the discussion with BlackRock, Standard Chartered and Robinhood executives in his X post.

First was the growing need for financial infrastructure that operates around the clock. Merrick noted that the next generation of digital asset markets will require always-on payment and settlement systems, rather than relying on traditional market opening hours. "The 24/7 reality: The next generation requires always-on rails. Markets with 'opening hours' just won't cut it," Merrick wrote.

The second point was about the importance of bridging traditional finance (TradFi) and decentralized finance (DeFi). Merrick said that to achieve growth in tokenized markets, institutions will need bank-grade infrastructure, reliable custody solutions and effective risk management systems to participate at scale.

"Bridging TradFi & DeFi: Scaling tokenized markets requires real bank-grade infrastructure, custody, and risk management," Merrick wrote.

#5175. Solana Microscope Offers Open-Source Monitoring for Complex On-Chain Operations

Strict editorial policy that focuses on accuracy, relevance, and impartiality

Morbi pretium leo et nisl aliquam mollis. Quisque arcu lorem, ultricies quis pellentesque nec, ullamcorper eu odio.

TL;DR: The Solana Foundation has described Microscope, an open-source monitoring approach for teams managing on-chain programmes and infrastructure. An alert is not a transaction blocker.

Operational tooling rarely generates the excitement of a new token launch, yet it can determine whether institutions trust a network enough to hold meaningful value on it. Teams want evidence that they can detect unexpected actions before the problem expands. Monitoring is one part of that process, not a substitute for preventive controls.

The Solana Foundation has described Microscope, an open-source monitoring approach for teams managing on-chain programmes and infrastructure. The toolkit is designed to help operators inspect instructions, transactions and alerts while connecting observations to common monitoring workflows. It is an engineering tool, not an announcement that every Solana application now receives automatic protection against attacks. The dated original disclosure is the starting point for the figures and product scope discussed here.

In a complex blockchain application, important activity can be hidden within technical transaction instructions that a general-purpose dashboard does not explain well. Monitoring tools try to decode those events and route relevant signals to people who can respond. This is useful for treasury movements, contract administration and unusual operational behaviour, but good alerts depend on choosing the right thresholds and maintaining the integrations.

The wider lesson is that on-chain transparency is not the same thing as operational comprehension. Transaction data may be public while a business still struggles to identify why an event matters or who should investigate it. A self-hostable tool can give teams more control over their monitoring stack, provided they manage uptime, access and alert delivery responsibly.

There is a meaningful gap between a fast transaction and a complete financial service. Solana infrastructure can settle digital assets, but an actual payments or securities product must also manage eligibility, legal rights, local currencies and exceptions. The best reporting follows the full workflow rather than assuming the chain’s speed is the entire product.

For additional perspective, it is worth reading our existing reporting on Solana’s Samsung Wallet rollout and user test and the latest Solana developer and payments coverage. They explain related market structure and should not be mistaken for evidence that every new proposal is already live.

An alert is not a transaction blocker. Microscope does not automatically fix a vulnerable programme, reverse an executed transfer or eliminate the need for security audits. False alarms can consume attention, while incomplete alert definitions can miss harmful activity. A monitored environment still requires tested incident procedures and clear accountability.

Distribution can be an advantage for blockchain applications, but the user experience needs to justify a change in behaviour. Developers also need monitoring and controls to support systems that operate continuously. A credible launch is one that describes available features, supported participants and how success will be measured after the initial announcement.

Watch for adoption in real applications, public documentation and integration examples showing how teams respond to alerts. The most useful evidence would involve measured detection speed and fewer unresolved incidents, rather than the number of dashboards deployed. For Solana developers, monitoring belongs alongside code review and permission design as a basic operating requirement.

#5174. Bitcoin miners’ daily revenue per unit of computing power falls 8.7%

Hashprice fell from $35.99 to $32.86 per PH/s/day in the November 2 weekly snapshot; October 30 forwards implied little network growth through December.

Bitcoin miners face weak dollar revenue even as the mining forward market priced in little additional competition through December, according to an October 30 analysis by mining data platform Hashrate Index. That could give miners a respite after difficulty’s 16.4% rebound.

Hashprice, the expected daily mining revenue for a unit of computing power, stood at $32.86 per petahash per second per day on November 2 at 18:00 UTC. That was down 8.7% from $35.99 a week earlier, according to the latest completed weekly snapshot.

Hashrate Index, Luxor’s mining data and research platform, put implied network computing power at 1,056 exahashes per second in November and 1,059 in December in its October 30 analysis. Both were near the approximately 1,062 EH/s average over the mining epoch that ended October 29.

Those are forward-implied levels, rather than observed future hashrate or scheduled difficulty changes. The linked September methodology assumes transaction fees of about 0.02 BTC per block when translating contract prices into implied network growth. Different fee outcomes can therefore change that interpretation.

The curve’s flattening followed a rise in difficulty from 127.45 trillion before September 19’s adjustment to 148.36 trillion on October 29. A pause would leave miners competing at that elevated level.

The November 2 update put average forward hashprice at $33.04 per PH/s/day for November through April 2027. This is an indicative revenue benchmark for that period, with actual execution dependent on agreed contract terms.

The October 30 curve implied nearly flat network growth. November 2’s revenue snapshot shows why a pause alone cannot repair margins.

The revenue rate moves with benchmark inputs, while power costs and actual output determine the margin.

A matched, performing hedge stabilizes the covered benchmark rate; operating costs and production remain exposed.

Stable competition and a forward hedge address different risks; neither fixes operating profit.

Curve levels are implications, not observed future hashrate. The November 2 snapshot is dated 18:00 UTC. Forward averages are indicative for November–April, not executable offers. Exposure diagrams assume matched quantities and dates and contract performance.

Reported values from October 30 and November 2. Network chart uses a labeled 1,000–1,100 EH/s scale. Hashprice bars start at zero and scale to reported values. Selectable diagrams explain sourced exposure mechanics without calculating profit.

Curve levels are implications, not observed future hashrate. The November 2 snapshot is dated 18:00 UTC. Forward averages are indicative for November–April, not executable offers. Exposure diagrams assume matched quantities and dates and contract performance.

A plateau in competition would limit additional pressure on revenue. Selling forward serves a separate purpose: stabilizing the benchmark revenue rate for output matched to the contract’s quantity and dates, if the contract performs.

In Luxor’s cash-settled contract, a seller receives the difference when the daily index falls below the agreed price and owes the difference when it rises above it. Matching the quantity and dates to actual output matters: the hedge does not ensure the machines keep producing.

A deliverable forward instead provides upfront buyer payment against delivery to Luxor’s mining pool, subject to seller credit checks and possible delivery margin. Luxor is the counterparty to both sides, and its order book displays buying and selling interest that its team must confirm as trades.

For operators with fixed dollar power costs, stabilizing the dollar revenue rate on matched output can improve cash-flow planning. Higher-cost fleets still need that revenue to cover electricity and other expenses; more efficient fleets have more room between gross receipts and costs.

Dollar hashprice also depends on Bitcoin’s price, fees and subsidy, alongside difficulty. Even if competition holds steady, weaker Bitcoin pricing or fees can reduce unhedged revenue.

#5173. Can XRP and RLUSD grow together? Here is Garlinghouse’s view!

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

For years, investors have debated whether RLUSD could take away some of XRP’s utility.

Since its launch, there has been some concern that XRP may fade as RLUSD develops more use cases in the real world. Major traditional finance players are also exploring RLUSD for cross-border payments and other financial activities, adding to this debate. Brad Garlinghouse’s latest comments help clarify Ripple’s position.

In a post on X, he mentioned XRP and RLUSD as two complementary assets that have different use cases. While RLUSD can be used for stablecoin transactions, XRP is still useful for providing liquidity and enabling cross border transactions. In short, both assets ultimately aim to expand Ripple’s payment network and drive more activity on the XRP Ledger.

However, the on-chain data presents a mixed picture.

According to DeFiLlama, RLUSD has seen its total supply exceed $2.5 billion for the first time. But the distribution across networks tells a different story. RLUSD supply on the XRP Ledger has dropped more than 30% to $850 million in the last 30 days. During that time, the supply of RLUSD on the Ethereum network has increased by more than 34% to $1.7 billion. This means that nearly 70% of RLUSD’s total supply has moved to the Ethereum network, while only a fraction exists on the XRP Ledger.

From an adoption standpoint, the on-chain trend adds another layer to the XRP-RLUSD debate. While RLUSD is gaining traction as a stablecoin, Ripple [XRP] wallets continue to attract large holders, suggesting that demand for both assets may be growing through different use cases rather than competing with each other.

The above data points to a clear shift in activity across the XRP Ledger ecosystem.

As previously mentioned, RLUSD supply within the XRPL network is falling. This could impact on-chain utility. As the amount of liquidity from the stablecoin falls, less RLUSD will be used for transaction purposes. However, XRP wallet data shows a different trend, with large holders continuing to add to their positions.

The chart below shows that wallets holding 1 million+ XRP have hit an all-time high for 2026. There are currently 2,147 XRP wallets containing a million tokens or more. This is up by 28 XRP wallets in just 10 days. In the last three days alone there have been 18 new XRP wallets added to the 1 million+ XRP holder category.

In essence, the on-chain data adds a new dimension to Brad Garlinghouse’s argument.

While the RLUSD liquidity on XRPL is decreasing, large holders appear to be increasing their XRP exposure, suggesting that investor interest is gradually shifting toward XRP. This divergence highlights an important change within the ecosystem. Although RLUSD continues to expand on Ethereum, XRP is seeing stronger accumulation among large wallets, potentially strengthening its position as a key asset within the XRPL.

RLUSD supply on XRPL fell 30%, while its Ethereum supply grew 34%.

XRP millionaire wallets hit a 2026 high of 2,147, showing growing interest from large holders.

#5171. Morgan Stanley Starts Tokenizing US Stocks and Treasuries via DTCC

Home » Crypto Market News » Morgan Stanley Starts Tokenizing US Stocks and Treasuries via DTCC

The bank confirmed it is among the first financial institutions to adopt DTCC’s tokenization service.

Morgan Stanley is taking a major step into modern market infrastructure by tokenizing U.S. equities and Treasuries through the Depository Trust & Clearing Corporation’s (DTCC) new Tokenization Service (DTS).

Following the platform’s official launch on October 28, the bank confirmed on X that it is actively using the system to create digital representations of traditional real-world assets.

This move brings Wall Street’s largest institutions closer to fully digital, on-chain trading infrastructure. More than 20 major financial firms—including JPMorgan and Citi—have signed agreements to participate

In its official X post, Morgan Stanley said it is “among the first banks to tokenize real-world assets” following the DTS launch. The bank said the service converts US equities and US Treasurys into tokenized form.

We are thrilled to announce that Morgan Stanley is among the first banks to tokenize real-world assets following the launch of the @The_DTCC Tokenization Service (DTS), converting U.S. Equity and U.S. Treasuries to tokenized form. Launched on October 28, DTS creates tokenized… pic.twitter.com/wUNMnYB5gC

According to the announcement, DTS creates tokenized representations of securities held in custody at the Depository Trust Company (DTC). Morgan Stanley said the goal is greater liquidity, collateral mobility, and capital efficiency.

DTS has been live since October 28 on the Canton Network and Hyperledger Besu blockchain. The service initially supports selected companies in the Russell 1000 index, ETFs tracking major indices, and US Treasury bills, notes, and bonds. The tokenized assets are designed to support 24/7 settlement, liquidity, and use as collateral.

According to DTCC, approximately $30 trillion in eligible securities may be covered by the initial rollout. More than 20 firms, including JPMorgan and Citi, have signed on to the service.

The tokenized real-world asset (RWA) market is worth $39.4 billion, with U.S. Treasuries accounting for more than $14.2 billion, according to RWA.xyz.

This institutional migration onto blockchain rails is made possible by clear regulatory framework updates. The U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission (SEC) issued a no-action letter to the DTCC, allowing its subsidiary DTC to operate tokenization services on approved blockchains under a three-year framework.

The SEC also approved rule updates allowing Nasdaq to roll out tokenized settlement options for Russell 1000 equities and major index-tracking ETFs, while work continues on broader digital asset innovation standards.

As the central backbone of U.S. market clearing, the DTCC oversees more than $114 trillion in assets under custody. Its adoption of public-enterprise hybrid blockchains marks a decisive shift from experimental pilot programs to fully regulated, mainstream financial operations.

#5169. US stock perpetuals win clearance, but stock-market halts can still block exits

Kalshi’s scheduled closures prevent opening and closing positions; Coinbase’s filed halt rules show how stock-linked perpetuals inherit equity-market restrictions.

The CFTC said on Nov. 3 that US stock perpetual futures could begin trading on eligible regulated exchanges. These contracts track share prices without a fixed expiration date, but holders remain subject to stock-market halt rules that can interrupt an exit.

Chairman Michael Selig said the contracts had been deemed approved under Regulation 40.3. Eligible venues are designated contract markets also notice-registered with the SEC as securities exchanges, bringing the products into the agencies' joint security-futures framework.

Kalshi says it now offers single-stock perpetuals, with no expiration date, but explicitly warns that they do not trade 24/7. Markets follow schedules with regular closures, and customers cannot open or close positions while a market is closed. Access requires a perpetuals application and educational tutorial; leverage depends on the stock and position size.

For Coinbase, the individual CFTC product record still displayed “Approval Pending” when checked Nov. 4, while the SEC's rulemaking index marked its separate customer-margin proposal withdrawn. Those entries do not overturn the Nov. 3 announcement, but neither establishes a Coinbase customer launch or final terms.

Coinbase's September filing describes cash-settled futures on stocks and ETF shares, without ownership of the underlying shares. Its product submission uses AAPL as a representative specification and makes listing contingent on approvals. Its proposed controls apply to Coinbase's design, rather than setting the terms for every venue.

Securities industry trade group SIFMA's Nov. 2 letter sought further review of proposals from Bitnomial, Coinbase, Kalshi and Nadex, raising concerns about manipulation, thin-liquidity trading and classification.

Under the security-futures halt requirement, a future on a single security must stop trading whenever that security is under a regulatory halt, subject to an applicable exemption. The agencies' joint-rule explanation says exemptions from both sets of requirements are needed.

These controls protect investors by allowing information to circulate and markets to regain order. Their immediate consequence is that an affected holder cannot use a closing trade in the halted contract to reduce exposure.

Buying or selling the same stock, or using its options, cannot be assumed to solve that problem: those markets generally honor the underlying regulatory halt too. A broader-market instrument might remain available during a company-specific interruption, but it leaves company-specific risk.

A venue-only derivatives outage presents a different case. If the cash market or another relevant instrument remains open and accessible, a separate trade may offset some exposure. That is a conditional hedge, dependent on access and liquidity, and it leaves the original perpetual open.

Coinbase's filed Rule 1207 would subject its contracts to US equity market-wide circuit breakers and coordinated halts, while also allowing restrictions for corporate actions, market disruptions and index-price or data outages. Reopening would follow the end of the halt condition and an exchange determination that resumption meets investor-protection and orderly-market requirements. The underlying stock reopening would not, by itself, promise an immediate futures exit.

Regulatory halts are subject to applicable exemptions. Hedge access and liquidity are conditional. Coinbase filings support the mechanism analysis, not a claim of current Coinbase launch or operative terms. Geometry is not time, price or probability.

Funding payments are cash adjustments on open positions. Changing their calculation is separate from executing a closing trade.

Coinbase's September product submission describes hourly funding and proposes trading from Sunday at 8 p.m. through Friday at 5 p.m. Eastern. Its index design checks input freshness, removes outliers, uses median pricing and falls back to a reference when inputs are insufficient.

The filed framework permits substitute price inputs and allows funding to be suspended, deferred or modified during disruption. The product appendix specifies no funding-rate publication for an entire hour in which trading is closed, paused or halted.

Neither a fallback price nor a funding pause closes the position or guarantees a trade at the reference value. Kalshi separately uses daily funding at 4 p.m. Eastern and warns that closures can leave holders facing a different reopening price. Adverse moves may push an account below maintenance margin and trigger liquidation.

#5168. Apple Joins 59-Company Strike to Freeze U.S. Stock Tokenization

Disclaimer: The opinions expressed by our writers are their own and do not represent the views of U.Today. The financial and market information provided on U.Today is intended for informational purposes only. U.Today is not liable for any financial losses incurred while trading cryptocurrencies. Conduct your own research by contacting financial experts before making any investment decisions. We believe that all content is accurate as of the date of publication, but certain offers mentioned may no longer be available.

Apple has officially blocked the launch of tokenized trading in its shares on the new American platform OKXICE TSV. Exercising its thirty-day veto right under the SEC's "innovation exception," the iPhone manufacturer supported a large-scale demarche by issuers.

By November 2026, the number of boycotting companies had reached 59, which effectively paralyzed the largest regulatory experiment on crossing Wall Street with the crypto market.

🚨NEW: Three more issuers have objected to @okx/@ICE_Markets tokenizing their shares, bringing the total to 59: Apple The Home Depot GE Vernova Six of the Magnificent Seven have now objected. Meta wasn’t on the original list.

The OKXICE TSV project — a joint venture between the crypto exchange OKX and Intercontinental Exchange, the owner of the NYSE — was preparing to launch round-the-clock on-chain trading through Uniswap v4 pools on the XLayer blockchain.

However, at the moment, the listing has been blocked by six of the seven members of the "Magnificent Seven" (all except Meta, which was not initially on the lists), as well as SpaceX, The Home Depot, GE Vernova, JPMorgan Chase and Goldman Sachs.

Insiders link this coordinated refusal to corporations' unwillingness to lose control over capital and to harsh behind-the-scenes pressure from the traditional exchange Nasdaq, which actively persuaded issuers to derail the initiative.

This internal boycott has exposed a deep regulatory gap: while the legal union of TradFi and DeFi on U.S. territory has been put on pause by the issuers themselves, international capital has long been trading digital analogs of U.S. securities outside the country.

By the end of 2026, when global Bitcoin ownership reached 4.5–6% of the planet's population, an autonomous blockchain liquidity had formed in offshore jurisdictions.

On foreign venues such as Kraken, Bybit and the international circuit of OKX, third-party versions of Apple shares are actively circulating, strictly closed to American residents. Among them, the leader in liquidity is the AAPLx token from Backed Finance on Solana and Ethereum with 1:1 custodial backing of shares, the tokenized AAPLon product from Ondo with automatic dividend reinvestment, as well as the new AAPL tokens from Backpack, subject to direct exchange for physical shares.

Corporate America, in the person of Apple, is defending its sovereignty in the home market, but the offshore market will not be stopped by this.

#5167. DeFi vault regulation splits Aave, Kamino and Veda from Morpho: Details

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

The DeFi industry is split on the best way for the U.S. SEC to regulate popular yield-generating crypto vaults.

On Tuesday, the 3rd of November, Aave, Solana-based Kamino, and crypto vault infrastructure firm Veda wrote to the SEC on how vaults should be treated.

Our argument: securities regulation should follow the rights, obligations, and powers an onchain arrangement actually creates. Who owns the assets? Who promises repayment? Who exercises control?

According to the trio, human discretion or active management by a DAO on crypto vaults should not automatically trigger the SEC’s securities laws. The lending protocols contend that, provided users still own assets and can withdraw them at will, such vaults can be deemed non-custodial.

For Aave, Kamino, and Veda, most vaults sit outside the current SEC’s security definition. This is slightly different from the approach sought by Morpho and the Crypto Council for Innovation (CCI).

Vaults are like managed funds such as mutual funds or ETFs, but on-chain and run by smart contracts. They allow users to have automated yield strategies to make their idle assets productive. Some are managed by risk curators like Steakhouse or Gauntlet, while others are fully run by code.

For the Morpho-backed CCI framework, some vaults run by risk curators could be deemed securities, because they act as managers who pick lending markets. In fact, even DAO-run protocols may partly be viewed as centralized managers and trigger securities law.

However, Aave, Kamino, and Veda disagree with some parts of this argument. For them, DAOs are for “governance, not management.” On the vaults managed by risk curators, Aave added,

A curator paid to make investment decisions about securities may be an investment adviser, but it does not have custody, because the code does not let it take the users’ assets.

That’s part of the user control and asset ownership argument the trio put across. In other words, the DeFi players agree that some of the current crypto vaults may be non-securities. But they split on how human intervention (risk curators and DAOs) impacts how the SEC should treat vaults.

The SEC did acknowledge that not all vaults are the same in a recent guideline. It will be interesting to see how the SEC addresses the human intervention question in vaults.

That said, vaults’ traction increased by 61% in H2, rising from $7B to $11B in assets under management (AUM), and are expected to explode alongside the tokenization boom.

#5166. Bitbase launches ORIGIN, distributing 1M points every week

Strict editorial policy that focuses on accuracy, relevance, and impartiality

Morbi pretium leo et nisl aliquam mollis. Quisque arcu lorem, ultricies quis pellentesque nec, ullamcorper eu odio.

Panama City, November 4, 2026 — Bitbase today launched ORIGIN, the first season of its new Bitbase Points program. Ahead of the launch, the digital asset exchange credited points to eligible users who had been trading on the platform, so their past activity counts from day one. The season runs for 26 weeks, until May 4, 2027, with 1,000,000 points distributed each week.

ORIGIN is also the first part of Bitbase 2.0 to reach users directly. Bitbase unveiled 2.0 on November 2, with an upgraded app and website and a new brand identity built around the statement “Market Flows. We Focus.” The idea is that markets will always move, and Bitbase chooses to focus on what lasts for its users. ORIGIN applies the same thinking to participation. Rather than rewarding a single burst of activity, it looks at what users do day after day over six months and credits the contribution they make across that period.

Bitbase built ORIGIN around what users already do on the exchange. Eligible users, new or existing, are enrolled automatically, with no separate registration. Points are allocated daily based on each participant’s contribution, which draws on four sources: trading, assets held on Bitbase, consistent activity and referrals. Because consistency is one of those sources, steady participation adds up over the course of the season.

ORIGIN also rewards users who bring others to Bitbase. A referrer receives an additional 25% of the contribution of each new user they refer directly, while the new user’s own contribution is unaffected.

Starting November 9, participants will also receive a shareable Trading Identity card that reflects their trading habits, with a new card issued every Monday until the season ends.

“Bitbase 2.0 is about focus, and for our users, that means knowing their steady participation is recognized and credited,” said Nicholas Lo, Chief Business Officer at Bitbase. “That’s why ORIGIN started by crediting what users had already done with us. It doesn’t ask anyone to trade differently. It simply records the participation that’s already there, every day, for the next six months.”

Users can track their points and rankings in the Bitbase app and on the website. Full details are available at bitbase.com/points.

Bitbase is a digital asset exchange serving individual and institutional users worldwide, with products spanning Spot, Futures, TradFi and Earn. Bitbase aims to give users a stable and straightforward trading experience, in line with its brand statement, “Market Flows. We Focus.” Learn more at bitbase.com.

#5165. Circle's USDC Dominates Stablecoin Market With 79% Share of Transaction Volume

Circle revealed that USDC accounted for 79% of the transaction volume of all stablecoins in the third quarter of 2026.

According to CEO Jeremy Allaire, the CEO of Circle explained that USDC's market share of the transaction volume of all stablecoins increased significantly from 66% in the same period a year ago. The overall USDC on-chain transaction volume was $13.9 trillion, an increase of 49% year-on-year.

This shows the rapid adoption of USDC in payments, trading, and blockchain-based financial applications despite stiff competition in the stablecoin space.

Circle stated that the issuance and redemption volumes of USDC in the third quarter reached a new high. In total, $208 billion of USDC was issued and redeemed in Q3. This translates to an average daily amount of $2.3 billion. For the third quarter, Circle said that the average USDC in circulation was $73.3 billion, which was up 18% year-on-year. This information was part of Circle's Q3 highlights.

Apart from USDC itself, Circle emphasized the growth in other aspects of its infrastructure. Circle indicated that Arc Mainnet went live in the third quarter. Over 100 institutions and applications are already building on Arc. As of Oct. 30, 2026, assets on Arc totaled $1.5 billion, as stated by Circle.

Circle Payment Network, or CPN, was another part of Circle's infrastructure that witnessed rapid growth. The company indicated that CPN ended the third quarter with an annualized payment volume of $41.3 billion. This was 15.2 times higher than in the same period last year, based on activity over the last 30 days. Allaire explained that CPN's payment volume is now more than $58 billion.

Circle also revealed that USDC has now become the leading settlement asset in agentic/automated internet payments. Allaire disclosed that over 99% of agent payments on x402 are settled in USDC.

In the third quarter, Circle's total revenue and reserve income were $865 million, up 17% year-on-year. This figure included $182 million from ARC token sales, according to an image showing Circle's Q3 highlights.

According to Allaire, the impressive performance in the third quarter is a strong signal that Circle is ready for the next phase of stablecoin adoption. He said the GENIUS Act, which is expected to take effect in January, will bring regulated digital dollars into the mainstream. Also, Circle has now activated all the layers of its platform.

The impressive third-quarter data indicates that Circle has not only further solidified USDC's dominance in terms of transaction activity but is also expanding its ecosystem across payments, on-chain finance, and institutional adoption.
