#5119. North Korea Denies $6.3B Binance Hack, Calls FBI Finding a 'Political Provocation'

North Korea Denies $6.3B Binance Hack, Calls FBI Finding a 'Political Provocation'

North Korea has rejected U.S. findings that it was behind the record hack of Binance, calling the FBI's attribution a "grave political provocation," Yonhap reported on Tuesday.

"It is a grave political provocation against the DPRK that the U.S. misleads the international public opinion as if all the cyber crimes in different parts of the world are our deeds," Kim Yo Jong, the sister of leader Kim Jong Un, said in a statement carried by the state-run Korean Central News Agency. She called the accusation a "sinister" attempt to damage North Korea's international image.

Kim also turned the blame back on Washington, pointing to the Trump administration's plan to use confiscated crypto as a strategic reserve. Anyone who looks at who will end up with "the so-called stolen cryptocurrency" once it is retrieved "may get a clue about who is behind the hacking incident," she said.

The FBI said on Saturday that North Korean hackers it calls TraderTraitor were responsible for the theft of more than $6 billion from Binance, confirming the attribution blockchain analytics firms TRM Labs and Chainalysis made last week. The FBI attributed the $1.5 billion hack of crypto exchange Bybit in February 2025 to the same group. North Korea has denied previous accusations of hacking.

The hackers are moving their haul quickly. North Korea is laundering the funds at a rate of roughly one new wallet every 25 seconds, across about 12,000 wallets, Decrypt reported on Monday. About $2.85 billion of the $6.43 billion stolen has been frozen or seized, compared with roughly 3% of the funds taken from Bybit.

Not all of the stolen funds are moving at the same pace. As of Monday, more than $1 billion in ETH and about $1.42 billion in BTC had seen limited onward movement, while other funds were moving rapidly across chains, blockchain analytics firm Chainalysis said. The activity so far is broadly consistent with the "immediate layering" phase seen after past North Korean hacks, though funds repeatedly returning to earlier addresses across thousands of transactions suggests automation, the firm said. It expects the scale of the theft to "strain the actors' trusted laundering channels," pushing funds toward bridges, instant exchanges and swapping services.

As of Nov 2, over $1B in ETH and ~$1.42 billion in BTC stolen from Binance have seen limited onward movement since the theft.

Other stolen funds are moving rapidly across chains.

These funds are not all progressing through the laundering process at the same pace. pic.twitter.com/ZEVCZzCDRM

— Chainalysis (@chainalysis) November 2, 2026

Binance did not respond to the BBC's questions about North Korea's alleged involvement. A company spokesperson told the outlet that a net $4.72 billion had been withdrawn since withdrawals resumed on Sunday, a "relatively limited portion" of total assets of about $157 billion.

By 10 p.m. ET on Monday, hourly deposits were exceeding withdrawals, "reflecting improving user activity and stabilizing flows," Binance tweeted. The exchange has since restored withdrawals for dozens more tokens on Ethereum and Solana.

Binance also said on Monday that users holding its 10 largest tokens, including BTC, ETH, USDT and USDC, can now verify that their balances are fully backed, using a zero-knowledge proof of its October 31 reserves snapshot. It has covered about 95% of the stolen assets, with the rest made up of less liquid tokens that take longer to buy, and plans to take a wider snapshot on Wednesday.

#5116. Justin Sun seeks sanctions against World Liberty, alleging Trump-linked firm hid UAE ties

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

The dispute over World Liberty's citizenship emerged after the company amended an initial court disclosure that had listed only one entity in its ownership chain and Florida as its sole citizenship.

Tron founder Justin Sun said Tuesday he has filed a motion seeking sanctions against World Liberty Financial, alleging the DeFi protocol failed to disclose its United Arab Emirates citizenship until months into their legal dispute.

Sun, who says he invested $45 million in WLFI tokens, sued the Trump-linked crypto venture in April, accusing it of fraud and unlawfully freezing his tokens.

The dispute over World Liberty's citizenship emerged after the company amended an initial court disclosure that had listed only one entity in its ownership chain and Florida as its sole citizenship. In a subsequent filing, World Liberty identified 22 members in its ownership chain and claimed citizenship in Florida, Delaware, Puerto Rico, and the UAE, according to Sun.

Sun alleges the late disclosure was an attempt to challenge the federal court's jurisdiction and push the case out of open court. World Liberty was "proudly" American when selling tokens, he said, but discovered its UAE roots "only when it needed a way out of an American federal courtroom."

Under federal diversity jurisdiction rules, Sun argued, his case could proceed in federal court only if he and World Liberty were citizens of different jurisdictions. Because a company takes on the citizenship of its owners for these purposes, World Liberty's UAE ties could undermine the court's jurisdiction.

Sun said he plans to refile the lawsuit in California state court after the sanctions motion is resolved.

In May, World Liberty Financial countersued Sun, accusing him of running a "meritless PR stunt" against the Trump-family-backed crypto project after his tokens were frozen.

World Liberty debuted in October 2024 amid significant scrutiny because of its association with President Donald Trump and his family. The WLFI token reached an all-time high of around $0.33 and currently trades near $0.051, giving it a fully diluted valuation of approximately $5.1 billion.

#5114. Huizenga Loses House Seat Despite $1 Million From Crypto PAC Fairshake

Rep. Bill Huizenga, vice chair of the House Financial Services Committee, lost despite $1 million from Fairshake.

Rep. Bill Huizenga, the No. 2 Republican on the House Financial Services Committee, lost his reelection bid to Democrat Sean McCann, the Associated Press called Wednesday afternoon, despite about $1 million in support from the crypto super PAC Fairshake.

With more than 97% of votes counted earlier today, McCann led Huizenga 50.1% to 48.3% in Michigan’s 4th Congressional District, a gap of about 7,000 votes out of nearly 300,000 cast. The race had been rated “lean Republican” heading into Tuesday.

Huizenga, an eight-term congressman and the committee’s vice chair, is the highest-ranking Republican on the panel to lose. He is not the only crypto ally on it to go down.

Rep. Bryan Steil of Wisconsin, who chairs the Subcommittee on Digital Assets, Financial Technology and Artificial Intelligence, lost to Democrat Mitchell Berman 52% to 48%. The AP called that race at 11:48 p.m. Tuesday.

Rep. Ann Wagner of Missouri, who chairs the capital markets subcommittee, lost to Democrat Fred Wellman. Rep. Zach Nunn of Iowa lost to state Sen. Sarah Trone Garriott, and Rep. Mike Lawler of New York conceded to Democrat Cait Conley before the AP called his race.

Fairshake committed at least $1 million in October to each of six House incumbents, among them Huizenga, Steil and committee Chairman French Hill of Arkansas. The super PAC reported spending $1,019,166 to support Huizenga in the general election, according to Federal Election Commission filings.

All 32 House incumbents the Fairshake network backed this fall voted for the CLARITY Act when it passed the House 294-134 in July 2025, CNBC reported. The network entered September with about $120.4 million in cash.

The crypto money was a small slice of the Huizenga race. Outside groups reported more than $25 million in spending in the district, FEC filings show, with House Majority PAC alone putting about $6 million against the Republican.

Two of Fairshake’s three Republican $1 million picks on the committee have now lost in midterms. Hill’s own race had not been called by the AP as of Wednesday afternoon. He led Democrat Chris Jones by about 4,400 votes, or 50.9% to 49.1%, with 99% of the expected vote counted.

Democrats won control of the House on Tuesday, which hands the committee gavel to their side regardless of Hill’s result. Rep. Maxine Waters of California, the panel’s top Democrat and its chair from 2019 to 2023, is in line to return to the post. Nineteen Financial Services Democrats backed her in October against a possible challenge from Rep. Sean Casten of Illinois, Politico reported.

Steil’s loss also leaves the digital assets subcommittee without its chair. Huizenga sat on that panel too. Journalist Eleanor Terrett noted that the subcommittee’s name, scope and membership are set each Congress, so Democrats could rename, re-scope or eliminate it.

Huizenga played a role in moving the stablecoin bill President Donald Trump signed in 2025, and he led the committee’s investigations into former SEC Chair Gary Gensler during the Biden administration.

The industry’s main legislative goal is still the CLARITY Act, which failed a Senate cloture vote 49-50 on Sept. 15. House Democrats are already weighing their own market structure bill with a bigger role for the SEC.

Crypto groups spent heavily to avoid this outcome, with about $176 million committed to more than 100 races this cycle. Losing Huizenga and Steil removes two of the Republicans who shaped crypto legislation on the committee, and both seats flip to Democrats.

Bitcoin itself rallied after the Democratic sweep, even as the industry’s allies in Congress lost ground.

#5113. Liquid Network Restores Full Bitcoin Backing Two Months After $320M Exploit

Liquid Network Restores Full Bitcoin Backing Two Months After $320M Exploit

The Liquid Network's Bitcoin reserves have been fully restored, two months after self-described white-hat hackers drained most of them, the Liquid Federation said on Monday.

The federation said it had reestablished 1:1 Bitcoin backing for all LBTC, the token that represents Bitcoin on Liquid. The milestone follows the restoration of swaps between BTC and LBTC at a 1:1 rate through participating providers several weeks ago, it said.

The Liquid Federation is pleased to announce that the network’s bitcoin reserves have been fully restored, reestablishing 1:1 BTC backing for all LBTC.

This follows the restoration of BTC<>LBTC swaps several weeks ago, which have since allowed…

— Liquid Network 🌊 (@Liquid_BTC) November 3, 2026

Blockstream CEO Adam Back, whose company develops the technology behind Liquid, posted a screenshot of the network's dashboard showing assets matching liabilities at 100%, with a reserve deficit of 0.00 BTC. Users running their own Liquid nodes can verify the federation's reserves directly, the federation said.

It did not say how the shortfall left by the hack was covered.

Launched by Blockstream in 2018, Liquid is a Bitcoin sidechain, a separate blockchain that runs alongside Bitcoin, designed for faster and more private transfers. Users lock up BTC in a wallet controlled by the federation, a group of exchanges and other crypto companies that run the network, and receive the same amount of LBTC in return.

Asked on X what would stop a repeat of the hack, Back listed three hardened "lines of defense": refreshed peg-out authorization keys now held in cold hardware wallets, disabled proof validation caches along with a bug fix for nodes, and consensus rules that have been hardened to "fail closed." The network has also been through multiple third-party audits, he said.

"And lesson: treat with suspicion (don't trust, verify) AI suggested fixes," Back wrote.

Back had pointed to AI's growing role in security in an interview with Bloomberg Television on October 20, describing "a wave of AI-assisted security analysis" over the previous couple of months. "AI, of course, is a tool to help develop code and to secure the code, but also it can be used to find defects," he said, adding that it had been "affecting many companies in the sector." He said then that the hacked funds remained "under investigation."

The federation said it is modernizing Liquid's governance structure, introducing clearer responsibilities and oversight, and adding further technical and operational safeguards, with more details to come in the coming weeks.

On September 6, purported white-hat hackers withdrew about 4,000 BTC, then worth around $320 million, from Liquid's federation wallet, communicating with Blockstream through PGP-signed messages embedded in Bitcoin transactions.

The next day, they returned 3,400 BTC, worth about $269 million, after Blockstream said Liquid's bridge nodes had been patched, but kept roughly 598 BTC. Ledger CTO Charles Guillemet said that if the remaining coins were a negotiated reward, the arrangement looked "more like extortion than white-hat hacking."

Blockstream later refused to pay a ransom for the outstanding Bitcoin. "Taking assets without authorization and withholding their return is a crime, not responsible disclosure," the company said. "It is not white-hat activity. It is theft."

#5109. Prediction markets bet on Democratic sweep of House, Senate in midterm elections

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

On Tuesday, Polymarket users gave Democrats a 76% chance of securing the Senate, and the House a 94% chance.

Ahead of polls closing in the U.S. midterm elections, bettors on prediction market platforms are increasingly pricing in Democrats taking both the House and Senate.

As of early Tuesday afternoon, Polymarket users gave Democrats a 76% chance of securing the Senate, and the House a 94% chance. Similarly, on Kalshi, bettors said Democrats had a 94% chance of controlling the House and a 75% chance for the Senate. All 435 House seats are up for election, as well as 35 of the 100 Senate seats.

Prediction markets surged in popularity during the 2024 presidential election, emerging as an alternative barometer of public opinion and drawing comparisons to national polling. Since then, platforms like Polymarket and Kalshi have generated significant revenue and reached multibillion-dollar valuations.

But interest in election-related trading has cooled considerably since 2024, according to a Bloomberg report published Tuesday. Politics accounted for just 1% of trading volume on both Kalshi and Polymarket in October, down from more than 60% during the 2024 election, Bloomberg reported.

A shift in parties is pivotal since Democrats are likely to then lead key committees, driving the agenda. For crypto, that could mean that Democrats such as Elizabeth Warren and Sherrod Brown could lead panels overseeing the digital asset industry.

Experts who spoke with The Block last month said that would mean more oversight over key issues, including President Donald Trump's crypto wealth through his family's World Liberty Financial, and less of an emphasis on crypto legislation.

As for key crypto races, such as Ohio, where incumbent Jon Husted will face off against crypto critic and former Sen. Brown, bettors on Kalshi say Brown has a 71% chance of beating Husted. For other races, including lawmakers focused on crypto legislation, Sen. Cory Booker, D.-N.J. has a 98% chance on Kalshi.

#5106. Judge Lets Stock Perpetual Futures Keep Trading but Says CFTC Likely Overstepped Its Authority

A federal judge refused to halt stock perpetual futures on Kalshi, Coinbase Derivatives and Bitnomial but found a Cboe- and Citadel Securities-backed group likely to prove the CFTC exceeded its authority.

Stock perpetual futures can keep trading on Kalshi, Coinbase Derivatives and Bitnomial for now after a federal judge denied an emergency bid to halt them Tuesday, while finding that the Wall Street-backed challengers are likely to prove the CFTC overstepped its authority.

U.S. District Judge Valerie Caproni denied the temporary restraining order on Nov. 3 after a teleconference with the parties, hours after the CFTC said the products could begin trading on U.S.-regulated exchanges.

The plaintiff is the Capital Markets Alliance Association, a New York nonprofit whose members include Cboe Global Markets, Citadel Securities and the Healthy Markets Association, according to a declaration from its secretary, Adam Ford.

The group sued the CFTC and Chairman Michael S. Selig in the Southern District of New York, case No. 1:26-cv-09608, seeking to undo the approvals of filings by all three exchanges.

Caproni wrote that the group “is likely to succeed on the merits” of its claim that the agency lacks statutory authority to allow perpetual derivatives on individual stocks to be offered to retail investors on CFTC-regulated platforms.

“Because the perpetual contracts, by definition, do not provide for a ‘sale for future delivery of a single security,’ they cannot be a security future over which the CFTC has statutory and regulatory authority,” she wrote.

Instead, she said, the contracts “appear to satisfy the definition of a swap,” which falls to the Securities and Exchange Commission.

She also found that the balance of equities and the public interest favor the alliance, writing that the agency’s actions “seem to represent a major disruption to the equities market.”

The request failed on one prong. Caproni said the declarations from Cboe, Citadel Securities and Healthy Markets were “conjectural” about the harm members would suffer and did not show irreparable harm that was “actual and imminent.”

The order leaves the approvals in place. It does not decide the case or the alliance’s separate motion for a preliminary injunction.

The CFTC’s opposition is due Friday, Nov. 6, and the alliance’s reply is due Tuesday, Nov. 10. The order says that schedule is subject to change by the assigned judge. The docket lists Judge Lorna G. Schofield as assigned to the case.

Kalshi was already listing four single-stock perps on Qualcomm, McDonald’s, Amgen and Costco by Wednesday morning, with maximum leverage of 4.9 times.

Bloomberg Law reported that the judge signaled she might ultimately side with the Wall Street firms opposed to the products.

The fight is over a label. Selig’s Nov. 3 statement said the contracts were deemed approved under Commission Regulation 40.3 as security futures products, a category the CFTC and SEC oversee jointly. The exchanges filed them Sept. 18, and the agency let its 45-day review period lapse without a written order.

The alliance argues that a contract with no expiration date and no delivery obligation is a security-based swap. Congress limited retail trading in those to national securities exchanges under full SEC oversight.

Its complaint says the CFTC “invited a parallel, synthetic market in U.S. stocks to grow outside the exclusive jurisdiction” of the SEC.

A CFTC spokesperson called the lawsuit “troubling” and told Reuters: “Our markets should be governed by law, not litigation campaigns intended to block innovation.”

For crypto-native venues, the stakes reach past stocks. Perpetuals are the core product of crypto derivatives trading, and Kalshi and Coinbase have used the CFTC’s framework to bring them onshore.

The agency approved the first U.S.-listed bitcoin perpetual on May 29, and Kalshi went live with the product soon after.

That approval already faces a challenge. CME Group sued the CFTC in June, arguing that a contract built on exchanged funding payments is a swap, not a future. That case is pending in Washington.

The New York order covers security futures, not commodities like bitcoin. But a federal judge has now said, at least preliminarily, that a contract with no expiration does not look like a future.

#5105. Binance Misses Monthly Luna Classic Burn for First Time Since 2023

Binance Misses Monthly Luna Classic Burn for First Time Since 2023

Centralized exchange Binance has burned Luna Classic tokens on the first of every month since 2023, when Do Kwon's Terra Luna empire came crumbling down. At the start of November, however, that burn has not occurred.

Luna Classic (LUNC) is the original native token for the Terra blockchain, which Terraform Labs founder Do Kwon forked and left behind in 2022 in a bid to rescue his collapsing project. Kwon was later convicted of fraud and sentenced to 15 years in prison for his role in Terra’s $40 billion implosion. Yet the decentralized network he left behind has kept running, propped up by a passionate community of supporters who have sunk time, effort and money into keeping it alive.

Binance started the burns after Terra Classic’s community voted to burn a portion of every on-chain LUNC transaction in late 2022, with the exchange pledging to burn half of the trading fees earned from the token. In practice, this has come in the form of a batch of burns happening at the start of every month since March 2023, but, according to StatsBin data, November's burn has yet to come in.

Some Luna Classic investors are concerned about Binance's lack of monthly burn, while others point to last week's record-breaking hack of the exchange as a reason behind the burn's delay.

"It is an important sign of support and a vital part of the overall burn campaign," Dawid Skinder, brand and user experience ecosystem contributor for Terra Classic, told Decrypt. "It might be that it is just an operational issue after the biggest hack ever. Hopefully that is the case, Terra Classic does not need another problem."

Binance was hacked for $6.3 billion on Friday, which the FBI has attributed to TraderTraitor—the North Korean group blamed for the Bybit hack. It has been labelled the biggest crypto hack in history, with estimated losses at four times the previous record of $1.5 billion from the Bybit heist.

The exchange has been reopening withdrawals on the platform in stages, with the latest taking place late on Monday for dozens of Ethereum and Solana tokens. Some investors have questioned whether the LUNC burn is a priority for the exchange as it attempts to recover from the hack.

— Tango and Cash (@ThisDud30190229) November 2, 2026

"Yes, the hack was a problem," prominent Luna Classic community member Pedro, better known as Vegas, told Decrypt. "At the moment, we are helping them to keep LUNC and LUNA safe."

He has long claimed to be in direct contact with Binance regarding LUNC issues, though this has yet to be verified. Vegas claims Binance is working on security maintenance after the hack and is moving LUNC to a more secure wallet before proceeding with the burn.

Binance did not respond to Decrypt's request for comment.

The exchange has paused its LUNC burns once before, withholding contributions at the end of 2022 after the Terra Classic community voted to re-mint burned tokens as a development fund. Binance halved its burn to 50% of trading fees and only resumed in March 2023, once the community had scrapped the re-mints.

Luna Classic has dropped 7.9% in value over the past week to $0.0000417, per Decrypt data, and is down 99.99% from its all-time high of $119.18. Investors believe the Binance burn being paused is just one of many issues the token faces.

Skinder told Decrypt that Luna Classic needs to fix its lack of products, improve its marketing, conduct a proper audit, and protect itself against quantum attacks. Skinder said he believes in a revival but claims that it is difficult with decentralized governance and "amateur validators."

So, why bother? Why not spend time on another chain?

"There are X number of chains in a better state than Terra Classic. None of them (except Bitcoin, probably) have that history and narrative (biggest crypto revival ever). None of them have 20+ stablecoins that are waiting to be re-pegged and restored," Skinder said. "Low chance of success, big upside if done. Great history to be part of."

#5102. CZ calls for crypto security alliance as AI-driven attacks accelerate after Binance hack

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

Changpeng Zhao says companies need AI-based defenses and an industry alliance to share lessons and freeze stolen funds faster, in his first interview since the Oct. 29 hack.

Binance cofounder and former CEO Changpeng "CZ" Zhao said AI is accelerating cyberattacks and argued companies should invest in AI-based defenses, security personnel, and partnerships with AI security providers.

In his first public interview since the Oct. 29 hack of Binance, Zhao told the Talking Tokens podcast that the threat extends beyond individual companies to the broader crypto industry and global financial system, given crypto's connections to stablecoins, tokenized stocks, real-world assets, and traditional finance.

"The speed of hacking is going to pick up because of AI capabilities [being] so strong now. At the same time, we've got to use more AI to defend. The only way to fight a monster is to kind of become that monster."

The comments come amid a string of security incidents across the crypto industry, including a separate AI-assisted breach at blockchain analytics firm Chainalysis and a $46 million security incident at Brazilian exchange Mercado Bitcoin.

Zhao said he learned of the incident around midnight in the UAE and focused on communicating with the community while Binance's team worked to contain the attack and protect users. He attributed relatively calm market conditions and subsequent net inflows to the company's response, financial capacity and user trust. He described security incidents as preventable with sufficient effort and attention, while warning that organizations can become complacent.

Earlier Tuesday, Binance said hackers used a fake job interview to compromise an employee's computer before leveraging an internal AI tool in the attack. The exchange also said it detected and prevented 57 suspected North Korea-linked cyber threats in 2026.

Zhao called for an industry alliance to share lessons and best practices, conduct cross-audits and help freeze stolen funds more quickly. He views security as an ongoing process rather than a milestone that must be reached before adoption.

"As an industry, I encourage [...] forming some kind of industry alliance on security to help each other learn from each other's mistakes, share the best practices, cross audits, help each other freeze funds very quickly, etc."

Zhao said he had not spoken with President Donald Trump or other U.S. officials about the incident, but that Binance and the broader crypto industry would welcome government assistance. Trump has said the U.S. was "looking into it."

"What's bad for crypto on this scale has real economic impact elsewhere," Zhao said. "So you cannot just say, 'Look, this is one specific problem for one specific sector or for one specific company.' It impacts the company, impacts the industry, impacts global finance. It's all interconnected, and I think, given the size of impact, it's definitely at the national security level as well."

Zhao expressed optimism that AI could help identify software vulnerabilities and strengthen defenses, while blockchain could support faster transactions facilitated by AI.

#5100. Morning Minute: Tom Lee Calls the Binance Hack Bullish for Ethereum

Morning Minute: Tom Lee Calls the Binance Hack Bullish for Ethereum

Morning Minute is a daily newsletter written by Tyler Warner. The analysis and opinions expressed are his own and do not necessarily reflect those of Decrypt. And check out our daily news show 'FOMO HOUR' covering all of the top stories and market action.

🐂 Tom Lee Calls the Binance Hack Bullish for Ethereum

Tom Lee looked at the largest crypto theft ever recorded and called the market’s response “constructive”. His company, BitMine Immersion, bought another 8,573 ETH last week, roughly $19.6 million worth, lifting the treasury to 6,060,506 ETH and about $13.7 billion. That’s 4.9% of the total circulating ETH supply, which makes Lee the most ETH-exposed executive on the planet.

His case comes down to one comparison. ETH fell about 4% after the $6.3 billion Binance hack. After the Bybit hack in February 2025, it dropped 8% on day one and 20% over the following week. Lee called that “relatively muted crypto market reaction very constructive” and read it as proof the industry can take a hit this size without cracking.

He pointed to four things behind the bullishness:

Each of those is a thing that went right in 2026 and went badly in 2025.

The price read holds up, but there are bigger questions for ETH’s future from a trading perspective. October was the worst month in the history of U.S. spot ETH ETFs, with $1.61 billion in net outflows. ETH is down 19% from its local peak, opposed to 11& for Bitcoin.

A potential driver of ETH’s weakness is Tom Lee himself. BitMine is now 99% of the way to the 5% supply cap Lee has said the company will stop at, about 49,500 ETH short, roughly six weeks at last week’s pace. ETH has had a large, price-insensitive, weekly corporate buyer for most of a year, and that buyer is nearly finished.

Now we wait to see what the bid looks like without it. And if Tom Lee’s bullish read is the correct one…

#5097. SEC division views Canton Coin as digital commodity in no-action letter

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

a16z partner Miles Jennings welcomed the SEC staff's position, arguing it could signal a broader regulatory path for blockchain network tokens.

The U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission's Division of Corporation Finance said it views Canton Coin (CC) as a digital commodity and will not recommend enforcement action over its programmatic issuance under certain conditions.

In a no-action letter published Tuesday, the division said Canton Coin derives its value from the programmatic operation of the Canton Network and supply-and-demand dynamics, rather than expectations of profit from the managerial efforts of others. It also cited the network's lack of a central party.

"Based on the facts presented, the Division will not recommend enforcement action to the Commission if, in reliance on your opinion as counsel, Canton Coin is minted programmatically by Super Validators, Validators and application providers, in the manner and under the circumstances described in your letter, without registration under Section 5 of the Securities Act or Section 12(g) of the Exchange Act," Deputy Chief Counsel Jonathan Ingram wrote.

The letter reflects the Division of Corporation Finance's enforcement position based on the specific facts and representations submitted by Canton and does not express a legal conclusion on the issues presented, including whether Canton Coin is a security.

In May, 21Shares became the first U.S. ETF issuer to offer direct exposure to CC, the network's native utility token.

In June, Digital Asset raised $355 million in a funding round led by Andreessen Horowitz's a16z crypto fund to expand the Canton ecosystem and accelerate institutional adoption of its blockchain infrastructure.

a16z partner Miles Jennings welcomed the SEC staff's position, arguing it could signal a broader regulatory path for blockchain network tokens.

"Incredible. @CantonNetwork just received no action relief from the SEC for its network token," Jennings wrote Tuesday on X. "The @SECGov's token taxonomy from March and follow up FAQs have now created a clear pathway out of securities laws—Network tokens are digital commodities."

#5095. Ethereum Privacy Network Aztec Rolls Out Major Security and Speed Upgrade

The Aztec Foundation said Tuesday that the upgrade, called Aztec Tierra and designated Alpha V6, delivers stronger security, formal verification of its proving system and faster deposits from Ethereum, alongside a refreshed brand.

Aztec is a decentralized layer-2 with more than 3,000 validators and fully programmable private smart contracts, and it pitches itself as having no privileged viewing keys or centralized sequencer, meaning no single operator can peer into or control user activity.

The upgrade resolves known vulnerabilities from earlier versions, including a critical issue discovered in V5, and adds extensive hardening. Notably, that includes a new AI-powered security testing harness and formal verification of core cryptographic components, a mathematical process for proving code behaves as intended.

The move reflects a broader scramble across crypto to harden code as AI tools make vulnerabilities easier to find.

On performance, Aztec said private transactions now generate client-side proofs in about one second on a standard device, twice as fast as before, while a new architecture cuts the time for Ethereum-to-Aztec messages from an average of two minutes to as little as 12 seconds, speeding up deposits and cross-chain activity.

The upgrade arrives with a crop of private applications spanning payments, trading, AI and social.

Leading them is zk.money, a private payments and savings app built by Aztec Labs that the team revived this year. With Tierra’s faster messaging, users can deposit directly from any Ethereum wallet without complex bridging.

Others in the lineup include Galactica for private AI inference, Nemi for private perpetuals trading, a private browser wallet called Nulo, and Rolling Chat, a private message board based on Ethereum co-founder Vitalik Buterin’s own Aztec experiments.

#5094. Moscow Exchange to launch Bitcoin, Ether and USDT trading in December

Moscow Exchange plans to launch crypto trading on Dec. 1 under Russia’s new law, with annual purchase limits for non-qualified investors.

Moscow Exchange plans to launch trading in Bitcoin, Ether and Tether’s USDT on Dec. 1, opening what it describes as Russia’s first organized digital currency market.

The exchange said Monday that pilot trades would begin as participants join the platform. Trades will use its existing stock-market infrastructure and be cleared through the National Clearing Center (NCC), with trading hours from 6:50 am to 11:50 pm Moscow time.

The venue will initially support same-day and next-day settlement, as well as swap transactions. Non-qualified investors must pass a test before purchasing crypto and will be limited to purchases of 300,000 rubles (about $3,700) annually per broker, according to the exchange.

The launch follows a Russian crypto law whose main provisions took effect Sept. 1. The law permits regulated trading but maintains a ban on cryptocurrency payments for goods and services in Russia.

Moscow Exchange and the NCC have been under US sanctions since June 2024, when the UK also sanctioned the exchange. The European Union added it to its sanctions list in July 2026.

Sergey Mendeleev, founder of Russian cross-border payments platform Exved, told Cointelegraph he doubted the proposed market would attract significant demand.

“Neither the global nor the local market will notice anything at all,” he said, questioning who would trade on a sanctioned exchange.

Mendeleev also predicted uncompetitive trading conditions, comparing the prospective offering to precious metals accounts at Russian lender Sberbank, where customers trade account balances without taking possession of physical metal.

Related: Russia to ban direct peer-to-peer crypto trades starting July 2027

He warned that investors could face “absolutely outrageous spreads,” referring to the difference between buying and selling prices. “For ordinary non-qualified investors, this is an entirely unattractive and unprofitable proposition,” he said.

Mendeleev saw potential for the platform to support cross-border payments. He said a venue where importers and exporters could buy and sell crypto directly with one another would be welcome, but questioned whether adding another intermediary would be competitive in a market where traders negotiate over price differences as small as 0.05%.

“I hope this will mark a genuine breakthrough for foreign trade,” he said.

#5091. South Korea’s ruling party formally proposes deferral of crypto income tax

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

If approved, the proposal would mark the fourth postponement of the levy, which was first legislated in 2020.

South Korea's ruling party leader has officially proposed that the government further delay the crypto tax.

Democratic Party leader Kim Min-seok said at a party supreme council meeting at the National Assembly that the decision followed consultations through the Strategy and Finance Committee, government-party talks, and meetings with crypto asset businesses, local news outlet News1 reported on Wednesday.

Kim stated that party members agreed that the country should first enact the Digital Asset Basic Act, a comprehensive legal framework for crypto, and give companies and investors time to adapt to new rules.

The tax plan, currently scheduled to take effect on Jan. 1, 2027, would impose a 22% tax (including local tax) on annual gains from the transfer or lending of crypto assets above a basic deduction of 2.5 million won ($1,868.7). If approved, the proposal would mark the fourth postponement of the levy, which was first legislated in 2020.

The ruling party leader acknowledged past deferrals of the tax and said that this time the party would set a clear deferral period while thoroughly preparing prerequisites, including use of overseas transaction information, IT system verification and taxpayer guidance.

Last week, South Korea's presidential office was reportedly discussing postponement of the levy until 2029 to align with the U.S. timeline for joining the OECD's Crypto-Asset Reporting Framework.

#5089. UK Says AI-Powered Ransomware Attack on Critical Infrastructure 'Highly Likely' by 2028

UK Says AI-Powered Ransomware Attack on Critical Infrastructure 'Highly Likely' by 2028

Britain's critical national infrastructure should be braced for a ransomware attack involving AI-powered malware by 2028, the UK's National Cyber Security Centre warned on Tuesday.

Within the next two years, hostile actors' use of AI is "highly likely" to increase the scale and efficiency of their operations, including ransomware, social engineering and the exploitation of software vulnerabilities, according to the NCSC's annual State of the Cyber Nation report. The agency is part of GCHQ, the UK's signals intelligence service.

Today, we've published our 2026 annual review: The State of the Cyber Nation.

The UK faces a moment of choice. The future of cyber resilience is not set.

Read the report now:https://t.co/TzqzYedqXQ@GCHQ @DanJarvisMBE pic.twitter.com/mIFWrBB5Yt

The threat stems less from new attack techniques than from old weaknesses, NCSC chief executive Richard Horne said in prepared remarks for a speech launching the report.

"In the coming months, we face a real prospect of an unprecedented rise in the number of cyber incidents," Horne said. "But we must be clear that is not because AI has invented new ways to attack us. But because vulnerabilities that have been tolerated for far too long can now be discovered and exploited at a scale that was once unimaginable."

"For decades, we have collectively got away with doing tech on the cheap," he added.

Horne urged organizations to invest properly in their technology, stop connecting systems to the internet unnecessarily, and treat the government's Cyber Essentials certification as the starting point for their security rather than the end goal. Leaders who fail to act could have to explain to investors, staff and customers why "food isn't on shelves, lights aren't on, businesses have gone under," he warned.

The government last week announced £60 million (about $80 million) for the first phase of a national Cyber Shield, £70 million ($93 million) for a new National Exposure and Real-Time Vulnerability Centre to identify weaknesses across critical infrastructure, and a new AI Cyber Security Centre of Excellence in Manchester, run by the NCSC.

The warning follows growing evidence that attackers are already putting AI to work. In May, Google's Threat Intelligence Group said cybercriminals had used an AI model to find and weaponize a previously unknown flaw that let them bypass two-factor authentication in a popular open-source administration tool, the first time it had confirmed AI-assisted zero-day development in the wild. A Cambridge University study of more than 90,000 cybercrime forum threads published the same month, however, found that most criminals were using AI for spam and phishing rather than sophisticated attacks.

UK officials have sounded the alarm before. In July, Foreign Secretary Yvette Cooper warned that governments must agree on AI safeguards before frontier systems transform warfare, crime and society. "We cannot afford to wait for an AI equivalent of Hiroshima before we act," she wrote.

#5086. What’s at stake for crypto as the US votes in the 2026 midterms?

Crypto groups spent about $176 million ahead of the US midterms, with control of Congress and the future of crypto legislation at stake.

US voters head to the polls in midterm elections on Tuesday that could shift control of Congress and shape the future of cryptocurrency regulation.

Crypto industry groups have spent roughly $176 million across more than 100 races this election cycle, according to an Oct. 27 Reuters report.

Coinbase, Ripple and Andreessen Horowitz are among the main financiers of the Fairshake political network.

The results could reshape the prospects for the stalled CLARITY Act and determine which lawmakers lead committees overseeing the industry.

Republicans enter Tuesday’s midterms controlling both chambers of Congress, with 219 seats in the House of Representatives and 53 in the Senate, according to the Blockchain Association’s election dashboard.

Democrats are seeking to overturn those majorities, with prediction markets favoring the party to win the House and Senate, according to Kalshi data at the time of publishing.

Voters will decide all 435 House seats and 35 Senate races, determining which party controls Congress for the final two years of President Donald Trump’s second term.

Democratic control could bring more congressional scrutiny of Trump-linked crypto businesses and regulators’ decisions. Control of the Senate would also affect confirmation of future presidential nominees to regulatory posts.

Ohio’s Senate race between Republican Jon Husted and Democrat Sherrod Brown is a major test of crypto’s political influence. Industry groups have reportedly spent more than $50 million opposing Brown, a longtime crypto critic who could return to a senior Senate committee role.

In Nebraska, Republican Sen. Pete Ricketts faces independent Dan Osborn. Ricketts, a Senate Banking Committee member who has supported crypto-friendly legislation, has benefited from nearly $11 million in spending by Fairshake, according to the Washington Examiner.

Texas is another race to watch, with Republican Ken Paxton facing Democrat James Talarico in a closely contested Senate election. As Cointelegraph previously reported, the Winklevoss-backed Digital Freedom Fund has spent money supporting Paxton.

A shift in congressional control could delay the CLARITY Act, although the crypto market structure bill has already struggled under Republican leadership.

The legislation would divide oversight of crypto markets between the Securities and Exchange Commission and the Commodity Futures Trading Commission, as well as establish rules for intermediaries. The Senate failed to advance it in a 49–50 procedural vote on Sept. 15, short of the 60 votes required.

Democratic Sen. Mark Warner cited insufficient safeguards against Trump profiting from his administration’s crypto policies. Republican sponsors said their final draft incorporated substantial ethics concessions sought by Democrats.

“A change in control of Congress would most likely reset the timetable for the CLARITY Act,” Christian Davies, group head of innovation and digital capital at Relm Insurance, told Cointelegraph.

Related: Crypto PAC spending before midterms possibly obscured after reported FEC hack

Ryan Kirkley, CEO of Global Settlement Network, agreed that the challenges extend beyond party control.

“The CLARITY Act was already struggling to get through the Senate with Republicans in control, so clearly the problem goes beyond which party holds the majority,” Kirkley said.

The industry has already secured a federal stablecoin framework through the GENIUS Act, which became law in July 2025.

Bitcoin’s (BTC) price history around US midterms has attracted attention on social media, but analysts caution that past declines offer little guidance on what could happen after Tuesday’s vote.

Bitcoin fell below earlier yearly lows after the 2014, 2018 and 2022 midterms. Source: ManGana

“I’d be careful reading much into Bitcoin’s record after past midterms. There haven’t been enough cycles to call it a pattern, and the market is built differently now: ETF flows, institutional portfolios and global liquidity move the price far more than they used to,” Michael Heinrich, CEO and co-founder of 0G Labs, told Cointelegraph.

Related: From Uptober to Hacktober: Are Bitcoin’s seasonal patterns becoming obsolete?

GSN’s Kirkley said institutional flows, interest rate expectations and market liquidity were more immediate factors affecting Bitcoin.

“Bitcoin doesn’t need a particular election result to succeed. It needs capital, confidence and a macro environment that gives investors a reason to take risk again,” Kirkley said.

#5085. Block Inc. shares rise 6% after-hours as it raises full-year profit forecast

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

Block raised its full-year gross profit forecast to $12.63 billion, which would represent a 22% year-on-year growth.

Jack Dorsey-led fintech firm Block Inc. Block, Inc. (XYZ) saw its shares jump 6.2% in after-hours trading on Tuesday after reporting stronger third-quarter earnings and raising its full-year profit outlook.

Block posted a net income of $566 million in the third quarter of this year, up from $462 million a year earlier, according to its latest quarterly report released Tuesday. It generated $6.72 billion in total revenue, up from $6.12 billion in the same quarter last year.

The company's gross profit rose 22% year-on-year to $3.24 billion, while its Cash App booked $2.07 billion in gross profit, up 27% year-on-year.

On the back of the results, Block raised its full-year gross profit forecast to $12.63 billion, representing a 22% year-on-year growth.

"We are raising our full-year guidance to reflect the momentum we are seeing across Block," the company said. Block said it expects gross profit to grow at least 15% in 2027, as it continues to "expand margins while still investing meaningfully to sustain long-term growth."

Block shares closed down 1.4% at $76 on Tuesday but jumped 6.2% in after-hours trading following the earnings release.

Meanwhile, the firm's bitcoin ecosystem gross profit fell 50% year-over-year to $52 million in the third quarter, according to the report.

It attributed the decline to its "strategic decision to reduce the fee" charged on certain bitcoin transactions on Cash App. Block's bitcoin ecosystem primarily consists of customer purchases of bitcoin within Cash App, Proto, as well as bitcoin withdrawal fees, per the report.

Cash App generated $4.07 billion in revenue in Q3, including $1.77 billion from the bitcoin ecosystem segment.

#5082. Epidemic of Hacked Freezers Spreads Across Europe

Epidemic of Hacked Freezers Spreads Across Europe

Europe's freezers have gone rogue, with supermarkets in nine more countries reporting refrigeration systems that heated up overnight on Monday.

Stores in Belgium, Estonia, Finland, Ireland, Italy, Luxembourg, the Netherlands, Portugal and Sweden reported problems, and Estonian units were pushed to 50°C (122 °F).

No group has claimed responsibility, so the only ones taking the heat are the freezers.

The outbreak has a patient zero in France, where Finance Minister Roland Lescure told Parliament on Oct. 13 that about 700 of the country's 10,000 supermarkets were affected.

According to France's CERT-FR, hackers forced a continuous defrost cycle, which spoiled food and sparked fires at some stores. CERT-FR said it was aware of numerous compromises of remote control modules made by Danfoss and Copeland.

Groupement Mousquetaires, which owns Intermarché and Netto, counted 300 stores affected, while Cooperative U said 80 of its 2,000 shops were hit. Lescure said food supplies were not at risk but acknowledged the attack "touches the heart of our food security." Some Mousquetaires display cases were still out of service that Tuesday.

Monday's wave hit several chains in Belgium, where Carrefour reported disruption at about 15 of its 700 stores. Sabcobel, a Belgian refrigeration contractor, said evaporators had been switched into defrost mode, and federal prosecutors opened an investigation. Four Delhaize outlets in Luxembourg also reported problems.

Italy's Radenza Group, which runs Coop-branded stores in Sicily, said the attack began at 4 a.m local time. and targeted the remote-control system for frozen food sections. Ireland's Musgrave counted 13 SuperValu stores affected, and Mosqueteiros confirmed incidents at seven Intermarché stores in Portugal.

At 50°C (122 °F), a freezer is well past hot-tub territory and turns basically into a low-powered steam oven or, worse, a bacteria incubator where germs grow quickly if food is left there too long.

In Sweden, Coop said six of its roughly 800 stores were hit and that they ran on 4G connections, making them "more exposed." Ica said a limited number of stores had problems and that the affected shops used an external Danfoss control system. Neither chain has confirmed a cyberattack, and Finland's Kesko says network interference has not been ruled out.

EU Agriculture Commissioner Christophe Hansen called the incidents a "deliberate attempt" to disrupt the food supply chain. Investigators have not said whether Monday's wave is linked to the French attack.

Estonian refrigeration firm Kitman Coldtech said the affected units were older Danfoss models similar to those attacked in France. CERT-FR warns that leaving an end-of-life device without security patches exposed to the internet carries a very high risk of compromise. Researchers at Claroty published flaws in Danfoss AK-SM 800A controllers in August, including an authentication bypass.

Claroty found 2,765 of those devices exposed online, and Danfoss fixed the flaws in firmware R4.3.1. It remains unclear which weaknesses the attackers used, and CERT-FR says the campaigns are probably still ongoing, while Italy's government was set to hold a cybersecurity meeting on Tuesday.

For now, CERT-FR advises operators to take the controllers off the public internet, and Sabcobel's CEO says many older units need physical modifications that require investment. Until those arrive, there is no real fix for the oldest units, which makes your 1998 chest freezer the most secure device on the continent.

#5079. Former Sen. Sherrod Brown defeats Jon Husted, dealing a blow to crypto PAC Fairshake

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

Earlier this year, Fairshake threw millions of dollars into the Ohio race, positioning itself against former Sen. Sherrod Brown.

Former Democratic Sen. Sherrod Brown is taking back his spot in the Senate, leaving crypto political action committee Fairshake with the bag as he beats out incumbent Republican Jon Husted.

On Tuesday night, Brown defeated Husted in the race for Ohio's Senate seat, dealing a setback to Fairshake, a top crypto political action committee that committed tens of millions of dollars to keep Brown out of Washington.

Earlier this year, Fairshake threw $30 million into the Ohio race, positioning itself against Brown. The crypto PAC and the broader crypto industry played a significant role in the 2024 elections, spending millions to influence political outcomes.

This is not Fairshake's first time spending against Brown. He was previously the chair of the Senate Banking Committee, where he set the agenda for crypto and called for a crackdown on the use of cryptocurrency to fund terrorism and evade sanctions.

Since Democrats regained control of the Senate, Brown could become chair of the Senate Agriculture Committee, which has jurisdiction over digital assets and prediction markets, among other things. If he is chair, sources previously told The Block that Brown would be unlikely to introduce any crypto legislation.

Other candidates backed by Stand With Crypto such as Democrat Chris Pappas in New Hampshire won their race.

Other pivotal Senate wins include Democratic Sens. Cory Booker and Mark Warner, both of whom have been a part of the negotiations for crypto legislation. Senate Democratic candidate in Texas, James Talarico, received an A rating from crypto advocacy group Stand With Crypto for his previous pro-crypto statements and his vote to create a strategic bitcoin reserve in Texas. Talarico also won his race on Tuesday against Texas Attorney General Ken Paxton.

Updated at 4:55 a.m. UTC to reflect that Democrats gained control of the Senate

#5076. Five Ways North Korea Breaks Into Crypto Firms—and How Binance Was Hacked

Five Ways North Korea Breaks Into Crypto Firms—and How Binance Was Hacked

Hackers from North Korea are notoriously effective at stealing crypto, with one estimate attributing 60% of all losses last year to DPRK-linked groups. These hackers enlist several different vectors of attack, making it all the more difficult to spot them in the act.

Last week, it took just 20 minutes for alleged North Korean hackers to drain $6.3 billion from Binance, in what is the biggest crypto hack on record. The attackers compromised a Binance engineer's work computer through a fake job interview, used an internal AI tool to gain control of other engineers' computers, and then used stolen credentials to hijack an internal wallet signing service, the exchange said.

In the past, North Korean hackers have been known to get hired at crypto companies under fake identities, attack through third-party infrastructure, and poison widely used code libraries. More recently, they've started using AI to make all of it more convincing.

Here are some examples of the methods that North Korean hackers are known to use in crypto attacks.

Binance chief security officer Jimmy Su told Decrypt in August 2025 that the exchange throws out suspected North Korean resumes every day, and that "almost all the large DPRK hacks," at the time, had involved a fake employee helping facilitate the attack.

Su said there were tell-tale signs in resumes that indicate the applicant was from North Korea, but the exchange was not willing to reveal the specifics on the red flags. Sometimes, though, malicious actors get through to the interview stage, where the attackers use deepfakes to change their face and voice.

"The only real good detection is that they almost always have a slow internet connection," he told Decrypt. "What's happening is that the translation and the voice changer are working during the call … that's why they are always delayed."

Those who do get hired tend to be suspiciously good at the job. Su said North Korean workers are often among a company's top performers, likely because several people may be sharing one role across time zones.

The threat has not let up. Binance said its security team detected and prevented 57 suspected North Korea-linked cyber threat cases in 2026, using techniques ranging from fraudulent job interviews to fake video conferencing apps.

Attackers also flip the scam around and pose as recruiters to target people who already work at high-profile crypto companies. Often, the attacker will claim there's a video or audio issue during the interview and send the victim a link to update Zoom, which infects their device with malware

"The DPRK group will [also] try to schedule calls with the external-facing employees," Su told Decrypt. "Either as a DeFi project or investment firm. Worst yet, they'll be recruiting them for a high-level job, paying twice, three times as much, just to get them onto an interview."

This is how the Binance attack began, according to the exchange. In September, a Binance software engineer was approached through a legitimate job-hunting platform by someone posing as a project director, who set up two interviews. In the first, a purported HR representative built trust over a live video call by sharing a social media account full of personal photos and videos, an approach Binance called "more advanced and deceptive than previously seen."

In the second, held on a chat app, the engineer was told to download an "assignment" onto his work computer. The code repository contained previously unknown malware that ran during setup, swapped a legitimate developer tool for a malicious version and connected to an attacker-controlled server, evading Binance's security tools.

One North Korea-linked group infected at least 30,000 devices in more than 100 countries between roughly December 2025 and July 2026 using similar methods. It took funds or credentials from more than 7,000 crypto wallets and moved around $10.71 million to North Korea.

The group, known as WaterPlum or Contagious Interview, posed as AI, crypto, or NFT companies and set candidates a technical interview or coding task that required downloading malware-filled files. Some used AI face-swapping software during interviews before cutting their video and asking the candidate to do the same, blaming a bad connection.

Binance's Su also told Decrypt that North Korean hackers poison public code libraries, ranking it as the second most common method of attack—behind fake employees.

Node Package Manager (NPM) packages are collections of reusable, open-source code that developers frequently use. Attackers can clone these packages and slip in a small line of malicious code while keeping its original function. That means that a victim could use this poisoned code library and not notice, with the malicious code embedding itself deeper over time as more is built on top of it.

In September 2025, software security firm ReversingLabs found two NPM packages that used Ethereum smart contracts to download malware, which the firm said it hadn't seen before. The packages were tied to a wider network of GitHub repositories, mostly branded as crypto trading bots, with thousands of commits and stars that ReversingLabs thinks were mostly faked.

The tactic formed part of a “sophisticated campaign” of malicious actors attempting to hack users via poisoned blockchain-related public code libraries, a vector of attack that Binance has previously attributed to North Korean hackers.

To counter the threat, Su said, Binance makes employees go through NPM libraries with a fine-tooth comb.

Tampered developer tools also played a part in the Binance hack. In June, a member of Binance's wallet DevOps team downloaded a tampered installation of Homebrew, a popular software installer, while trying to install an AI coding tool he had found through a search engine, the exchange said. Its security team caught the problem, replaced the device and rotated his credentials, but the incident exposed the engineer and his colleagues on the wallet infrastructure team to the attackers.

Before Binance, the unwanted record for the largest crypto hack belonged to Bybit, which lost $1.4 billion in February 2025. Cybersecurity firms revealed that North Korean hackers pulled off the attack by planting malicious code into the infrastructure of Safe—a crypto wallet provider used by the exchange.

The security firms said malicious JavaScript code was directly injected into Safe's online infrastructure. Though, it was unclear how the attackers managed to do this.

Bybit's infrastructure "was not compromised," the exchange told Decrypt, and the hack came purely because a third party was exploited.

The Binance attack targeted the exchange's own systems. On October 29, the attacker used stolen credentials to deploy a container and gain unauthorized access to an internal wallet signing service, which let it initiate transactions outside Binance's normal withdrawal process. On Ethereum, it used the signing service to exploit EIP-7702, a mechanism that lets an account delegate execution rights to a smart contract, giving an attacker-controlled contract partial control over a Binance hot wallet.

Those on-chain authorizations kept working even after Binance suspended withdrawals. Because the hot wallet used multi-party computation, there was no single private key Binance could use to revoke them quickly, the exchange said.

Every single one of the above mentioned attack vectors can be turbo-charged with artificial intelligence tools.

Early this year, Google warned of an AI-powered malware campaign targeting crypto companies coming from North Korea. Similarly, Binance's Su also mentioned that AI tools can be used to increase the effectiveness of attacks.

Concerns around AI-powered hacks have risen in recent months, with Ethereum co-founder Vitalik Buterin saying in October that it's "plausible" AI could break encryption within the next two years. It came amid an explosion in AI-driven maths, with OpenAI claiming to have cracked a 90-year-old maths problem in a matter of days. Experts urged the industry to prepare for AI-powered attacks on encryption.

There are no confirmed reports of North Korean hackers using AI to break encryption. But given how quickly they've adopted AI elsewhere, the industry may have to prepare for that next.

#5073. French bank Crédit Mutuel Arkéa exploited for over $133 million, ZachXBT says

ZachXBT said he has a 'high degree of confidence' that the attacker is not associated with North Korea's Lazarus Group.

Onchain investigator ZachXBT has identified French cooperative bank Crédit Mutuel Arkéa as the alleged victim of a theft estimated at more than $133 million.

ZachXBT first reported on Nov. 2 that an unnamed EU entity had lost over $106 million from multiple centralized exchange accounts the previous day, with funds withdrawn as USDC and USDT.

He has criticized the institution for allegedly allowing more than $30 million to be laundered, noting that proper incident disclosure to EU law enforcement could have frozen the funds.

Crédit Mutuel Arkéa has not issued any public statement confirming or denying the incident. The Block has reached out to the bank but has not heard back. The claims remain based on ZachXBT's own analysis and have not been independently verified by the bank or authorities.

The bank has more than 5.6 million customers, with total assets of 211.5 billion euros (around $237 billion).

#5072. No, You’re Not Crazy. Seasonal Allergies Can Mess With Your Skin

Here’s how to keep things under control and glowing.

Women's Health may earn commission from the links on this page, but we only feature products we believe in. Why Trust Us?

YOU PROBABLY KNOW THE USUAL SIGNS of seasonal allergies: sneezing, congestion, itchy eyes, the seemingly endless need for tissues. But what about sudden dry patches on your face? Or puffiness around your eyes that no amount of concealer seems to hide? Turns out, your skin might be having an allergic reaction of its own this time of year.

And you wouldn’t be the only one. Roughly one in four American adults experiences seasonal allergies, according to data from the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention. While we tend to associate them with respiratory symptoms, the same immune response responsible for a runny nose can also cause your complexion to go haywire. "Seasonal allergies affect the skin through both the immune response itself and the habits that come with feeling itchy and congested," says Dendy Engelman, MD, a board-certified dermatologist. That means the irritation you're seeing in the mirror might not be entirely unrelated to what's floating around outside—you might be overdoing it with the Kleenex or making yourself even puffier with lack of sleep. (Joy.)

Meet the experts: Dendy Engelman, MD, is a board-certified dermatologist with the Shafer Clinic in New York City. Daniel Gardner, MD, is a board-certified plastic surgeon and chief medical officer at NakedMD. Mona Gohara, MD, is a board-certified dermatologist and clinical professor of dermatology at Yale University.

The good news? Understanding what's happening beneath the surface can help you manage the symptoms. Ahead, experts explain how and why seasonal allergies can wreak havoc on your complexion, ways to recognize the signs, and what actually helps.

Seasonal allergies, sometimes called hay fever or seasonal allergic rhinitis, occur when your immune system reacts to otherwise harmless environmental substances, such as pollen from trees, grasses, and weeds. Unlike food allergies, which involve reactions to something you've eaten, or insect-sting allergies, seasonal allergies are typically triggered by allergens circulating in the air during particular times of year.

The underlying immune response, however, isn't entirely different. When someone who's sensitized to pollen encounters it, their immune system can trigger specialized cells called mast cells to release histamine and other inflammatory chemicals. "Histamine, in response to seasonal allergies, is our body making a mountain out of a molehill," says Daniel Gardner, MD, a board-certified plastic surgeon and chief medical officer at NakedMD. "Essentially, our body is sensing danger that it doesn't really exist."

Histamine contributes to itching, inflammation, and increased permeability of small blood vessels, allowing fluid to move into surrounding tissues. It's a useful defense mechanism in the right circumstances, but during allergy season, it can leave you feeling (and looking) pretty uncomfortable.

While not everyone with seasonal allergies will experience skin symptoms, certain people are especially susceptible, particularly those with eczema or an underlying tendency toward allergic conditions. There’s even a name for it—the “atopic triad,” which refers to the relationship between eczema, asthma, and seasonal allergies, says Mona Gohara, MD, a board-certified dermatologist and clinical professor of dermatology at Yale University.

People with these overlapping conditions may be especially susceptible to experiencing dry, itchy, inflamed skin, particularly on the face and hands. Here's what they—and you—may notice:

One of the most recognizable effects is swelling around the delicate eye area. Histamine increases blood vessel permeability, allowing fluid to accumulate in surrounding tissue. This can exaggerate the natural contours beneath your eyes, sometimes making existing under-eye bags appear more prominent, says Dr. Gardner.

Allergies can also indirectly compromise your skin barrier, the outermost protective layer of skin that’s responsible for keeping moisture in and irritants out. Repeatedly rubbing itchy eyes, wiping your nose, or scratching irritated skin can weaken that barrier, leading to further dryness and inflammation.

For people already prone to eczema, seasonal allergens can make existing symptoms worse. Patches may become particularly dry and itchy, especially around the eyes, face, and hands. Chronic eczema can accentuate the appearance of fine lines, meaning seasonal flare-ups may temporarily make skin look more textured, Dr. Gohara adds.

Histamine can stimulate nerve endings associated with itching and, in some allergic reactions, trigger raised, swollen welts known as hives. However, hives aren't specific to pollen allergies and can have numerous other causes.

Of course, not every mysterious rash is attributable to the season. Dr. Engelman recommends considering when symptoms appear, where they're located, and whether they coincide with sneezing or watery eyes. "Does the irritation return at the same time each year? Is it worse after being outdoors? Are you also sneezing or experiencing itchy, watery eyes?" she asks. "Those clues make seasonal allergies more likely but do not establish a diagnosis."

Dr. Gohara agrees that understanding the pattern is essential, particularly when skin symptoms overlap with other conditions. "Timing is everything," she says, and recommends working with an allergist to identify potential triggers and determine whether seasonal exposures correspond with skin flare-ups.

A reaction that develops after applying a particular skincare product, meanwhile, might indicate contact dermatitis. And yes, it's possible to develop an allergy to a product you've used without issue for years.

The bad news: There's no single skincare product that can magically make you look flawless if you’re experiencing an allergic response. The most effective approach typically involves treating the underlying allergies while protecting your skin from additional irritation. For starters, oral antihistamines, particularly second-generation options such as cetirizine or loratadine, can help manage histamine-driven symptoms, including itching and hives. However, Dr. Engelman cautions that they won't necessarily resolve eczema-related inflammation or repair a damaged skin barrier.

Dr. Gohara emphasizes that identifying the actual cause of irritation is equally important. She recommends getting evaluated early to distinguish seasonal allergies from other potential causes of skin irritation and determine which treatments are appropriate. For those experiencing persistent itching or eczema, prescription treatments may be necessary, rather than relying exclusively on over-the-counter allergy medications.

If itchy eyes are your main complaint, antihistamine eye drops containing olopatadine or ketotifen may offer relief when used as directed. Addressing the itching can also help break the cycle of rubbing and irritating the surrounding skin. For more persistent seasonal symptoms, an allergist may recommend testing, medications, or allergen immunotherapy, commonly called allergy shots.

As for your skincare routine? Less is generally more when your skin is already inflamed. Engelman recommends sticking with a gentle, fragrance-free cleanser, moisturizer, and well-tolerated broad-spectrum SPF 30 or higher, while temporarily avoiding retinoids, exfoliating acids, and scrubs on irritated areas. Gohara similarly recommends gentle that you gotta keep that skin hydrated, adding that barrier-repairing creams can be particularly beneficial when allergy-related irritation has compromised the skin's protective barrier. Helpful ingredients include glycerin, which attracts moisture, and petrolatum, which reduces water loss from dry patches.

A clean, cool compress can also soothe irritated skin around the eyes without the additional friction of rubbing. And while you might be tempted to book a facial or other treatment to tackle visible irritation, some procedures are better postponed. "I would postpone chemical peels, microneedling, or resurfacing procedures over actively irritated or eczematous skin," says Dr. Engelman. "Adding a procedure that disrupts the barrier can make recovery more difficult."

When it comes to soft-touch washes that get the job, nothing holds a candle to this Cerave classic. Chock full of healing ceramides and hydrating glycerin, it’s a foaming cleanser that won’t leave skin stripped.

An all-around superstar, this dermatologist-developed cream is ideally suited to sensitized skin. What’s in there? Hydrating glycerin, a barrier-boosting peptide complex, and soothing ceramides.

A zinc oxide-based formula that provides gentle protection from UV damage and oxidative stress. This one comes in five shades to flatter most skin tones without leaving behind a white cast. It’s also fragrance- and oil-free.

This Korean cult favorite is for those days when your skin needs a little extra support—slather it on to keep hydration in and irritation at bay. A nourishing blend of ectoin, ceramide NP, and hylaronic acid fortify your outer layer.

If your complexion seems to have a mind of its own whenever pollen counts rise, seasonal allergies could be contributing to the problem. But while occasional puffiness or irritation may improve with at-home care and allergy treatments, persistent itching, worsening eczema, or recurring rashes are worth bringing up with a dermatologist. In this case, your doctor may recommend prescription anti-inflammatory treatments or, in certain cases, medically supervised phototherapy.

In general, it’s always best not to go it alone—especially true if you're not sure what's causing the reaction in the first place. Skincare ingredients, environmental irritants, and underlying skin conditions can produce remarkably similar symptoms. A dermatologist can help distinguish between them, recommend appropriate treatments, and determine whether additional allergy testing is necessary.

It's also worth remembering that allergies aren't necessarily something you're born with. Sensitivities can develop at any age, says Dr. Gohara, even to substances you've previously tolerated. What worked for your skin last spring might not necessarily work this year.

Ultimately, taking care of your skin during allergy season isn't about piling on more products or chasing a quick cosmetic fix. It's about identifying what's triggering the inflammation, treating the allergy when necessary, and giving your skin barrier a chance to recover.

#5070. Elon Musk’s Tesla and SpaceX Block Their Stock From OKX-ICE Tokenized Venue

Elon Musk’s Tesla and SpaceX Block Their Stock From OKX-ICE Tokenized Venue

Two of Elon Musk’s companies are now keeping their shares off OKX and ICE’s planned tokenized stock venue.

Tesla and SpaceX are among six new objecting issuers listed in an updated notice from OKXICE TSV dated Monday. Amazon, Netflix, Lam Research, and SoFi Technologies are the others. The additions bring the total to 56 companies.

The list stood at 50 in the venue’s Oct. 30 notice, when Nvidia, Costco, and Nike led the biggest batch yet. That followed 38 on Oct. 28, after Walmart joined, and nine on Oct. 15, when the first objectors appeared.

The new names deepen the roster of megacap holdouts. Amazon, Tesla, and Netflix join Microsoft, Alphabet, and Nvidia, while SoFi, a fintech company that offers crypto trading, adds a company with its own stake in digital assets. SpaceX, which trades under the ticker SPCX, rounds out the batch.

Under the SEC’s innovation exemption, which the agency unveiled in September after the Clarity Act stalled in the Senate, a venue must notify a company before offering a version of its shares tokenized by an unaffiliated third party.

If the company objects in writing within 30 days, the venue can’t make that stock available for trading, according to the SEC’s order. The objection applies only to that venue.

OKXICE TSV, a 50-50 joint venture between NYSE owner Intercontinental Exchange and OKX’s U.S. holding company, plans to offer tokenized U.S. stocks 24/7 through permissioned Uniswap v4 pools on OKX’s XLayer blockchain, paired with stablecoins such as USDC.

To trade, users must pass identity and sanctions checks and hold a nontransferable “soulbound” token that the venue’s smart contracts verify before every transaction.

Apple is now one of the few megacap names still missing from the list. And while objections bar OKXICE from listing third-party tokenized versions of a company’s stock, they don’t close every door.

The notice says the venue can also list shares tokenized by the issuers themselves, though it doesn’t identify any, and that tokenized stocks it offers aren’t restricted to trading on OKXICE alone.

#5067. Morgan Stanley among first banks to tokenize US equities, Treasurys through DTCC’s service

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

The bank is among the first to use DTCC's new tokenization service as it separately prepares to support tokenized equities in Q1 2027.

Morgan Stanley has begun using the Depository Trust & Clearing Corporation's new tokenization infrastructure to tokenize real-world assets, joining a group of financial institutions that accessed the service after its launch last week.

The bank said it has tokenized U.S. equities and Treasurys through the service, making it among the first to do so, according to an X post.

"The hands-on experience of integrating tokenized securities with established market infrastructure will inform the continued development of the firm's digital asset capabilities as the market evolves," said Amy Oldenburg, Morgan Stanley's head of digital assets strategy.

DTC, DTCC's depository subsidiary, which holds $114 trillion in assets under custody, signed 20 participants to access the service at launch, including Bank of America, Barclays, and BNP Paribas. Citi, Fidelity Investments, JPMorgan, State Street, and UBS were also listed, with plans to onboard future users on a rolling basis put in place.

DTCC said at the time the service was developed with input from more than 150 firms and initially supported Canton and its private Besu network.

The development comes as Morgan Stanley expects to support tokenized equities on its alternative trading system in the first quarter of 2027, later than its previous H2 2026 target. Oldenburg told The Block last month that the bank was building the necessary infrastructure for the initiative.

Meanwhile, Morgan Stanley is not alone in the tokenization race. The New York Stock Exchange and its parent, Intercontinental Exchange, are also advancing plans for a tokenized trading platform. Broadridge, Computershare, Equiniti, and Superstate have signed agreements to serve as transfer agents.
