#5066. Brazil's Biggest Bitcoin Exchange Says It Will Refund Users After a $40 Million Hack

Brazil's Biggest Bitcoin Exchange Says It Will Refund Users After a $40 Million Hack

Brazil's largest crypto exchange says its users won't pay for its hack. Mercado Bitcoin, which lost tens of millions of dollars on Monday, says the losses are its problem, not its customers'.

An exchange holds your coins the way a bank holds your cash, and when one gets robbed, the customers are often the ones left waiting to see what they get back.

The exchange spotted a breach early on November 2 and froze deposits and withdrawals. “We identified and quickly contained a security incident in one of our systems early this morning,” Mercado Bitcoin said in a Portuguese-language statement on X. “As a preventive protection measure, deposits and withdrawals are temporarily suspended.”

It said no customer lost money and no data leaked. It has not said how the attackers got in.

How much was taken depends on who is counting. Security firm Hypernative flagged more than $40 million in suspicious transfers across at least 10 blockchains, including Bitcoin, Ethereum, Solana, and Tron.

Primeiro, o mais importante: os ativos dos clientes estão garantidos . Os saldos continuam registrados nas respectivas contas.

Ontem (2/11), identificamos um incidente de segurança. Acionamos na hora nossos protocolos de segurança e governança e informamos as autoridades.…

— Mercado Bitcoin (@MercadoBitcoin) November 3, 2026

The exchange's own number is lower. “Right now the value of the assets involved is about $38 million,” Mercado Bitcoin said in a Portuguese-language update on X. “After a quick action with the crypto community and strategic partners, including Tether, Circle, Paxos, BRL1 and Chainalysis, we have already blocked $11 million.”

Others put it higher. Blockchain analysts at Arkham traced at least $46 million, while Chainalysis says about $49 million, with nearly $10 million frozen. The exchange has not said whether its $38 million includes the frozen amount.

Tether, which issues the USDT stablecoin (a token pegged to the dollar), froze more than $4.5 million in USDT tied to the incident, CEO Paolo Ardoino said on X.

A freeze only stops the tokens from moving. It doesn't return them to their owner, which is why the exchange's promise to cover losses carries the weight here.

On October 29, hackers drained more than $6 billion from Binance, the world's biggest exchange, and the FBI blamed North Korea's TraderTraitor group. Mercado Bitcoin has not linked its incident to that attack.

Binance made the same pledge as Mercado Bitcoin. “Binance will absorb the financial impact of the stolen funds, not our users,” CEO Richard Teng wrote on X, adding that about 95% of the affected assets had been replenished.

Neither promise can be checked from the outside. A guarantee to cover losses is only as good as the money behind it, and exchanges don't have to show customers their books.

For now, Mercado Bitcoin deposits and withdrawals remain suspended with no restart date. The exchange says it will post updates on X and resume service once full security is restored.

#5064. Weighted Daggers. Sword Yoga. Real-Life Gauntlets. Welcome to the World of Romantasy-Inspired Fitness.

Women's Health may earn commission from the links on this page, but we only feature products we believe in. Why Trust Us?

You’ll still break a sweat, but with “the added bonus of feeling badass.”

The female warrior lunges, her sword slashing in the sunlit forest. Clad in black armor, she spins, whirling her weapon over her head. Silver slices the air. She jumps, spins, then kicks. No, this isn’t a Valkyrie training scene from the newest installment in Sarah J. Maas’s hit romantasy (romantic fantasy) series, A Court of Splintered Harmony. It’s a workout class.

Charity LeBlanc didn’t set out to create a romantasy-inspired workout program. Originally, the former American Ninja Warrior semifinalist just wanted to share videos of her fitness journey and fantasy-inspired cosplay creations on social media—and her follower count grew. Then, in 2024, she heard about the Dragon Gauntlet, a Fourth Wing-inspired obstacle course race in Boyne Falls, Michigan. She asked her followers if they wanted to train together for the event, and designed a workout program that combined strength training, HIIT cardio, combat circuits, and weapons training. “It wasn't just working out to do a rep; it was to gain a skill,” says LeBlanc. “And it took off.”

Romantasy-inspired workout programs have exploded over the last few years, mirroring the genre’s takeover of the publishing industry. After reading A Court of Thorns and Roses in 2022, trainer Samantha Willens put her Harry Potter- and Star Wars-themed Pilates classes on pause and shifted her focus to workouts inspired by the bestselling romantasy series. She created the Valkyrie Squad Program, a four-month fitness plan based on the series’ fifth book, A Court of Silver Flames—and her business, Micro Squad, grew exponentially.

In 2024, Sabina Storberg launched WeaponUp, a sword yoga platform, thinking it would attract women into traditional martial arts or yoga. But “it was actually the book girls,” she says. Now, they have thousands of members around the world.

Sure, gaining the endurance of a dragon rider, the agility of a half-Fae assassin, or the muscle of a Valkyrie warrior (IYKYK!) isn’t your average fitness goal, but that’s why these workouts work. Romantasy books allow women to fall in love with stories of female empowerment—and these workouts allow women to live out those stories in real life. “There's something to be said for channeling a character you admire, especially one who embodies strength, confidence, and resilience,” says exercise physiologist Rachelle Reed, PhD. “For some women, that connection might make exercise feel more fun and approachable, or even help them feel more confident trying something new.”

Here’s how women are taking on their own version of a heroine’s journey—and what mixing sweat with a little bit of magic has done for their minds and bodies.

Jamie Dalton, 38, never really enjoyed working out, especially in front of other people. She always struggled with coordination, which made her self-conscious. “Weight training was the closest to me enjoying something because that required the least amount of coordination,” Dalton says. “But it just felt more like a punishment, and punishments, you don't keep doing long-term.” She’d often start a workout program only to stop a few months later.

But when Dalton discovered WeaponUp, her relationship with fitness transformed. The classes consist of sword drills and choreographed flows inspired by romantasy characters, like Nesta Archeron from ACOTAR and Manon Blackbeak from Throne of Glass. And while the workouts are rooted in fantasy, the moves are a real fusion of kung-fu and yoga, and involve a lot of squats, lunges, kicks, core work, balance, and mobility. They also present a mental challenge as you learn to wield a sword with each hand and on either side of the body. Five months in, and Dalton is still going strong with WeaponUp, training four to five times a week.

While working out wasn’t totally foreign to Dalton, many of the people who sign up for WeaponUp, Whimsy Forge, and Micro Squad are new to exercise. Romantasy-inspired fitness “lowers the barrier to entry” because it speaks to people’s existing interests and feels personalized; they can put themselves in the shoes of the strong female protagonist(s) they’ve admired in book form, Reed says.

For instance, all of Whimsy Forge’s eight-week workout plans, called character arcs, are designed to emulate a different fantasy character's abilities, whether that's an Elven Huntress’ agility, a Dragon Rider’s endurance, or a Valkyrie Warrior’s strength. Whichever workout plan you choose, you’ll still do classic exercise moves, such as squats and presses, as well as optional special skill challenges that require a (play) weapon. “I'm really passionate about making sure the programs are accessible, and I am not a gym girl,” says LeBlanc. “For a lot of the workouts, all you really need is a yoga mat and some space.”

Both the level of access and the genuine enthusiasm for the subject matter are enough to help some people get started with fitness, which is often the hardest part. “When people are new exercisers, if they have an enjoyable or interesting experience the first few times, it's easier for them to start building toward a habit,” says Reed. “Eventually, your discipline and intrinsic motivation pick up.”

Of course, romantasy-inspired fitness can also help more experienced exercisers get out of a workout rut. Brooke Lydon, 33, used to love taking fitness classes, but between a full-time job and raising her 4-year-old daughter, she didn’t have the time. A fan of yoga teacher Taylor Johnson, Lydon thought that doing her fantasy-inspired strength and mobility program, Arius, might be a fun way to get back into fitness, and she liked that she could do the program at home.

Plus, it gave Lydon an extra opportunity to listen to her romantasy audiobooks during workouts, which doubled as a form of motivation. She’d often imagine herself competing against her favorite characters and end up working harder. “I would be like, ‘If she can do it, I have to do it,’” she says.

Romantasy-inspired fitness may offer an extra incentive to exercise, but it’s also just plain fun. Each program infuses a sense of childlike joy and wonder into workouts. “We don't have that many fun fitness experiences available to us—everything is for a goal,” Storberg says. “I'm going to go to the gym so I gain muscle, and that's very important, but we don't make it fun like we used to when we were children.”

Can a romantasy workout provide the same level of results as traditional strength training or cardio? For Dalton, who was losing muscle and experiencing joint pain due to perimenopause, the answer is a resounding yes. “I definitely feel like I've regained some of the mobility that I felt like I was losing, and I've definitely regained a lot of the strength back,” she says.

WeaponUp classes have also helped Dalton improve her coordination, thanks to the focus on proprioception (the understanding of where your body is in space). “You're doing your whole flow on a yoga mat, and you might have your kids' stuff in one corner, so you have to have a really good understanding of where you are in space, where the sword is in space,” says Storberg.

But sword yoga also provides balance training in a more accessible way, especially since you have a prop. “When I try to do a squat normally, I feel like I'm going to fall over,” Dalton says. “But because of the flow that they have you do with a sword, somehow it counterbalances so I feel more grounded and secure.”

Meanwhile, in Micro Squad’s Pilates-based classes, you’ll do moves like Russian twist punches with (optional) Dragon’s Edge weighted daggers that evoke a character or pivotal scene from ACOTAR through the series called “A Fortnight in Prythian.” As such, you’ll experience many of the same muscle endurance benefits that come from any low-weight, high-rep workout. But there’s also “the added bonus of feeling badass while wielding weighted daggers and immersing yourself in the world,” Willens says.

Additionally, in the Valkyrie Squad program, you’ll train foundational strength and mobility skills before progressing to martial arts, kickboxing drills, and sword training, like the warrior characters in A Court of Silver Flames. Many Micro Squad members finish the program feeling not only confident in their bodies, but also like they can take on any obstacle—physical or mental, Willens says.

It’s no coincidence that a fitness program based in fantasy can lead to real-life gains. If you can build confidence with a class you find interesting, you’re more likely to stick with it for the long-term, Reed says. In fact, several WeaponUp members have lost 50 pounds or more after starting the program. “That got them into a trajectory of getting into wellness [and] being able to really take their health back,” Storberg adds. “There's so many women that are actually sticking to a fitness routine because it's fun, unique, and different.”

Badassery is the intangible benefit that all romantasy-themed workouts seem to share. “A lot of women have never seen themselves as this person who is strong, who is capable, who can do skills, but walking into these programs where they feel like they're becoming something, that transformation is real for them,” LeBlanc says. “They actually are becoming stronger.”

With the popularity of romantasy fitness only growing, it’s also evident that more women are invested in what their bodies can do, not what they look like. For as much as the average romantasy female main character may uphold traditional beauty standards, that’s not why millions of readers love them, or why thousands want to work out like them. “It's not just about getting a smaller waist or growing glutes. A lot of women's fitness is about how you look and less about how it makes you feel,” says LeBlanc. “[This is] a lot more motivating and empowering.”

Romantasy-inspired fitness has even helped Dalton navigate discussions with her 7-year-old daughter about exercise, food, and health in general. “It's made it a lot easier to turn that conversation into becoming strong and powerful, instead of skinny,” she says. Her daughter will often join her for WeaponUp workouts, and “that has been a really cool thing for me as a mom,” Dalton says. “I want my kid to exercise in a way that's going to help her build healthy habits, and I love that this is letting me do that with her.”

Fantasy fitness is becoming more than just a workout trend—it’s a way for fans to connect beyond the pages of their favorite books. Whimsy Forge hosts a semi-annual Trial of the Guilds, a community-wide virtual fitness challenge where members choose one of the aforementioned character arcs, and everyone in the same arc becomes a Guild, or team. Then, all the Guilds compete against each other for eight weeks. “We do it as a community, so you feel like you're with friends and people that are going to encourage you,” says LeBlanc.

In addition to online fitness programming, several of these platforms now offer in-person classes and meetups. The WeaponUp team just finished a 10-city Graceful Warrior tour that took them from coast to coast, attracting a range of ages, sizes, and fitness abilities, says Storberg: “What they have in common is this desire to be the main character.” That desire is so strong for Dalton that she’s willing to drive three or four hours to attend an in-person WeaponUp event. “[I’d] go and make a whole day of it,” she says.

The collective power of the book girls, both online and in-person, is not to be underestimated. “Having a community can provide accountability, encouragement, and a sense of belonging, which can all make it easier to stick with an exercise routine over time,” says Reed. “But what's particularly interesting here is that these women are connecting over more than just exercise. They already have a shared interest in the books, characters, and stories, which gives them another way to connect with one another.” (Perhaps unsurprisingly, Whimsy Forge, WeaponUp, and Micro Squad all boast virtual book clubs.)

While many women are initially drawn to romantasy fitness because of a connection to a character, they not only forge new connections with other readers, but ultimately, themselves. “It’s less about acting like some of these female main characters that we see in the books, but rather becoming a female main character in your own life,” says Storberg. After all, who needs a shadow daddy when you can swing a sword?

Lindsay Geller is the lifestyle director at Women’s Health, where she oversees the Sex & Love, Relationships, and Life sections. She has more than 10 years of experience covering sex, relationships, health, fitness, and general lifestyle topics for print and digital publications. She is also a NASM-certified personal trainer and lives in New Jersey with her husband, daughter, and rescue dog.

#5063. Binance traces $6.3B hack to fake job interviews, compromised AI tool

Hackers exploited compromised employee computers and stolen credentials to breach Binance’s wallet infrastructure, according to the exchange’s investigation.

A coding assignment from a prospective employer seemed like an ordinary part of a job interview. Instead, it was one step in a months-long cyberattack that ended with $6.3 billion stolen from Binance, raising questions about how exchanges protect themselves against threats that target their employees.

The attack was part of a coordinated campaign that exploited both human vulnerabilities and internal security systems, according to a postmortem released by Binance on Tuesday. The exchange’s findings detailed how hackers gradually gained access to its wallet infrastructure before the Oct. 29 theft.

According to Binance’s security report, the attack campaign began in June, when an engineer downloaded a tampered software package while setting up an AI coding tool. Although Binance detected the malware and replaced the affected device, the attackers had already gathered information about the exchange’s wallet team.

In September, attackers posing as recruiters targeted another software engineer and tricked the employee into downloading a malicious file disguised as a coding assignment. The malware compromised the engineer’s work computer and evaded the exchange’s security protections.

On Oct. 20, the attackers used the compromised computer and an internal AI tool to gain access to two additional engineers’s systems, obtaining deployment credentials that allowed them to install malicious software within Binance’s wallet infrastructure on Oct. 29.

The attackers then gained access to the exchange’s internal wallet-signing service, which authorizes cryptocurrency transactions. This allowed them to move funds outside the normal withdrawal process.

“This wasn’t one mistake or one breach — it was a months-long campaign,” Binance co-CEO Richard Teng said in a Tuesday post on X.

Binance said the attackers also exploited Ethereum’s EIP-7702 mechanism, which allows wallets to delegate transaction permissions to smart contracts, enabling unauthorized transfers to continue even after the exchange suspended withdrawals. Its multiparty computation (MPC) wallet system, which distributes control over transaction signing, complicated efforts to revoke those permissions.

The breach affected approximately $6.3 billion in digital assets, with Binance reporting that $2.85 billion had been frozen or intercepted, according to its latest accounting. The exchange said it would absorb the losses rather than pass them on to customers and had replenished 98% of the affected token assets as of Oct. 31.

The attack, the largest cryptocurrency hack in history, has been attributed to North Korean hackers by the Federal Bureau of Investigation (FBI). On Tuesday, Kim Yo Jong, the sister of North Korean leader Kim Jong Un, denied the country’s involvement, saying the FBI’s attribution was politically motivated and intended to damage North Korea’s international standing.

Binance co-founder Yi He said Tuesday that the exchange’s security team had detected and prevented 57 suspected North Korea-linked cyber threats in 2026, including fraudulent job interviews, malicious software packages and social engineering attempts aimed at stealing credentials or installing malware.

“We intercepted many of them, but the cost of failing to stop even one was severe—and this should never have happened,” she said.

Magazine: Binance hackers raced to move billions before it could be frozen

#5061. DeFi Explained

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

Understanding How Decentralized Finance Protocols Work

DeFi, or decentralized finance, works through smart contracts that coordinate financial activity on-chain. Instead of relying on a centralized intermediary, DeFi protocols use liquidity pools, automated market makers, collateral rules, oracles, governance systems, and composable protocol integrations to support trading, lending, yield strategies, derivatives, and other financial functions.

DeFi 201 is intended for readers who already understand crypto basics and want to understand how DeFi protocols actually work. It moves beyond beginner definitions and into the systems that keep decentralized finance running: exchanges, lending markets, yield strategies, oracle networks, derivatives, governance, and composability.

DeFi is best understood as a connected protocol system rather than a list of isolated apps. Its main components include decentralized exchanges, lending protocols, yield aggregators, oracles, decentralized derivatives, governance, and composability.

how decentralized exchanges enable on-chain trading through order books, AMMs, liquidity pools, and intent-based execution;

how DeFi lending markets use collateral, pooled liquidity, utilization, interest rates, and liquidations;

how yield aggregators use vaults, strategy routing, managers, and keepers to automate DeFi strategies;

why oracles are critical for prices, collateral values, liquidations, derivatives, and synthetic assets;

how decentralized derivatives create synthetic exposure through collateral, margin, funding, and liquidation mechanics;

how DAOs and governance tokens coordinate proposals, voting, delegation, treasuries, and protocol upgrades;

how composability lets protocols build on each other while creating dependency and systemic-risk trade-offs.

Explain how DEX architectures route trades and allocate liquidity.

Describe the mechanics and risks of DeFi lending protocols.

Explain the role of vaults and automated yield strategies.

Identify oracle dependencies and manipulation risk.

Describe how DeFi protocols package derivative exposure without centralized intermediaries.

Explain how DAOs coordinate protocol upgrades and incentives.

Analyze both the benefits and risks of DeFi composability.

The lesson summaries below are a short overview of the full course content. Take the full interactive DeFi 201 course to explore each lesson in more depth through guided explanations, examples, and activities.

Compares on-chain order books, constant-product, concentrated-liquidity, constant-sum, and hybrid AMMs with intent-based routing by competing solvers.

Follows collateralized borrowing through pooled liquidity, utilization-based rates, supply and borrow APY, health factors, and liquidation mechanics.

Shows how vaults route deposits across strategies and automate compounding while adding smart-contract, strategy, fee, governance, and automation risk.

Explains how smart contracts obtain price and other external data and why source quality, aggregation, latency, manipulation resistance, and failure modes matter across DeFi.

Introduces perpetuals, options, and synthetic assets and connects their design to collateral, margin, funding, liquidation, oracle, and leverage risks.

Covers governance tokens, proposals, delegation, quorum, voting power, treasuries, protocol upgrades, and the risks of low participation or concentrated control.

Explains how protocols combine as money legos to create new products while dependencies can transmit smart-contract, oracle, collateral, bridge, or governance failures across DeFi.

DeFi means decentralized finance. It refers to financial protocols that run through blockchains and smart contracts rather than traditional intermediaries. In DeFi, users can trade, lend, borrow, supply liquidity, manage collateral, vote on protocol changes, or build products by connecting multiple protocols together.

The important idea is not that DeFi removes every kind of intermediation. It changes where coordination happens. Rules that would usually be enforced by a platform, broker, exchange, or bank can be encoded into smart contracts, governance systems, oracle feeds, and liquidity mechanisms.

Most DeFi protocols combine a few common building blocks: users, smart contracts, liquidity providers, traders, borrowers, lenders, governance participants, and external data providers. The exact mix depends on the protocol type.

Smart contracts: Encode the rules for trading, lending, collateral, fees, voting, or settlement.

Liquidity pools: Hold assets that traders or borrowers interact with.

Liquidity providers: Supply assets to pools and take protocol-specific risks.

Oracles: Bring external data, such as prices, into smart contracts.

Governance: Coordinates upgrades, parameters, incentives, and treasury decisions.

Composability: Lets protocols plug into each other and create new products.

Decentralized exchanges allow users to trade on-chain through smart contracts. Early exchange design borrowed from order books, where buyers and sellers post bids and asks. But on-chain order books can be expensive and capital-inefficient because each order update consumes gas and liquidity may sit idle.

Automated market makers changed that model. In an AMM, traders do not need to match with another person’s order. They trade against a shared liquidity pool supplied by liquidity providers. A liquidity pool is a smart contract that holds tokens, and an algorithmic formula determines prices based on the pool’s reserves.

The constant product AMM model uses the formula x times y equals k. It keeps the product of the two token reserves constant, so prices move automatically as traders buy one asset and add another. This model is simple and resilient, but it spreads liquidity across all possible prices, which can make capital inefficient.

Core idea: Matches bids and asks directly.

Trade-off: Costly and capital-inefficient on-chain.

Strength: Simple, always provides liquidity.

Trade-off: Capital may sit unused far from market price.

Strength: Higher capital efficiency and tighter spreads.

Trade-off: Requires more active liquidity management.

Core idea: Keeps exchange rate near fixed parity.

Trade-off: Can break if one side of the pool is drained.

Core idea: Blends product and sum curves.

Strength: Useful for stable or pegged assets.

Trade-off: More complex and less intuitive.

Core idea: Users express desired outcomes; solvers compete to fulfill them.

Strength: Can improve routing and user experience.

Trade-off: Depends on solver competition and execution quality.

Liquidity providers deposit assets into pools so other users can trade. In return, LPs may earn a share of trading fees. But providing liquidity is not passive income without risk. LP returns depend on trading volume, fee tiers, price movement, pool design, and impermanent loss.

LP profitability can be expressed as trading fees earned minus impermanent loss, if any.

DeFi lending protocols let users supply assets to earn yield and borrow against collateral without a traditional bank. Smart contracts replace bilateral lending relationships with pooled markets that set rules, manage collateral, and enforce liquidations automatically.

A typical lending protocol uses overcollateralization. A borrower deposits collateral, borrows a smaller value against it, and must keep the position healthy. If collateral value falls too far, the protocol can liquidate the position to protect the lending pool.

Collateral: Assets pledged to secure a loan or leveraged position.

Utilization: How much of a lending pool is currently borrowed.

Supply APY: The rate suppliers may earn for lending assets.

Borrow APY: The rate borrowers pay to use borrowed assets.

Health factor: A measure of whether a position is safely collateralized.

Liquidation: Forced sale or closure when a position becomes unsafe.

Yield aggregators simplify DeFi yield generation by routing deposits across strategies and automating parts of the process. Instead of manually moving funds between pools or protocols, users deposit into a vault. The aggregator then follows a strategy, compounds returns, and abstracts away some of the operational work.

This does not remove risk. Aggregators add another layer of smart-contract exposure, strategy risk, fee policy, governance risk, and dependence on keepers or automation agents. Yield optimization is a mechanism to understand, not a recommendation.

DeFi protocols often need information that does not naturally live on-chain. Lending markets need asset prices to value collateral. Derivatives need reference prices. Synthetic assets need external data. Liquidations depend on timely and accurate price information.

Oracles provide this data to smart contracts. Because smart contracts can execute automatically based on oracle inputs, oracle design is a core security issue. Bad data, delayed data, manipulation, or concentrated data sources can create losses across trading, lending, derivatives, and liquidation systems.

More resilient designs combine multiple sources and aggregate observations before publishing a value on-chain. That can reduce dependence on one provider, but it does not eliminate latency, source correlation, or governance risk.

Decentralized derivatives create exposure to assets or market outcomes without necessarily requiring users to hold the underlying asset directly. Common examples include perpetual futures, options, synthetic assets, and other collateralized instruments.

The key mechanics include collateral, margin, funding rates, liquidation thresholds, and protocol risk controls. Perpetual futures have no expiry and use funding payments to help keep contract prices aligned with spot prices. Like other DeFi products, derivatives can increase capital efficiency but also introduce leverage, liquidation, oracle, and systemic-risk concerns.

Many DeFi protocols are governed through DAOs, governance tokens, proposals, voting systems, delegation, and treasuries. Governance determines how protocols update parameters, allocate incentives, manage upgrades, and respond to risk.

Proposals may need a quorum or other participation threshold before a vote can take effect. The exact rules determine how voting power is counted, how delegates act, and how quickly a protocol can respond to changing conditions.

Governance can make protocols more community-driven, but it can also create concentration and coordination problems. Token-weighted voting may give large holders outsized influence. Low voter participation can make decisions fragile. Delegation can improve expertise but may also centralize practical control.

Composability is the ability for DeFi protocols to plug into and build on top of one another. This is why DeFi is often described as money legos. A lending protocol can use an oracle, a yield aggregator can route deposits across lending markets, and a derivatives protocol can depend on multiple liquidity and data systems.

Composability accelerates innovation because developers can reuse existing liquidity, infrastructure, and protocol primitives. But it also creates dependency risk. If one widely used protocol, oracle, bridge, collateral asset, or governance decision fails, the effects can cascade across many connected applications.

What it does: Enables on-chain trading through pools, curves, routing, or intents.

Main risk: Slippage, LP risk, smart-contract risk, solver/routing risk.

What it does: Lets users supply assets and borrow against collateral.

Main risk: Liquidation, collateral volatility, oracle risk.

What it does: Automates deposits, routing, strategies, and compounding.

Main risk: Strategy risk, contract risk, fee/governance risk.

What it does: Supplies external data to smart contracts.

Main risk: Data manipulation, latency, source concentration.

What it does: Creates synthetic or leveraged exposure.

Main risk: Funding, liquidation, margin, oracle, and leverage risk.

What it does: Coordinates protocol decisions and upgrades.

Main risk: Voter concentration, low participation, governance capture.

What it does: Connects multiple protocols into new products.

Main risk: Dependency and cascading failure risk.

DeFi replaces some intermediaries with code, liquidity systems, and governance. That does not remove risk. It changes the form of risk.

Smart contract risk: code can contain bugs or be exploited.

Oracle risk: bad or delayed data can trigger wrong pricing, lending, or liquidation outcomes.

Liquidity risk: pools can become shallow, imbalanced, or expensive to trade against.

Liquidation risk: collateralized positions can be closed when markets move quickly.

Governance risk: token voting can be captured, delayed, or dominated by large holders.

Composability risk: protocols that depend on one another can transmit failures across the system.

Regulatory risk: DeFi protocols, interfaces, and participants may face changing legal treatment.

This page is for educational purposes and explains protocol mechanics. It does not provide investment, trading, tax, or legal advice.

A user wants to trade ETH for USDC through a decentralized exchange. Instead of matching with a specific seller, the user trades against an ETH/USDC liquidity pool. Liquidity providers have deposited both assets into the pool. The smart contract calculates the trade based on the pool’s pricing formula, updates the token reserves, charges a trading fee, and sends the output token to the user.

The user gets an on-chain swap. LPs earn fees. The pool’s price changes because its reserves changed. If the trade is large relative to pool liquidity, the user may experience slippage. If the pool is part of a larger aggregator or intent-based route, solvers or routers may search across multiple liquidity sources to improve execution.

DeFi protocols use smart contracts to coordinate financial activity without a traditional centralized intermediary.

DEXs and AMMs use liquidity pools and algorithmic pricing to support on-chain trading.

DeFi lending protocols use collateral, utilization, rates, and liquidations to manage risk.

Yield aggregators automate strategy routing and compounding, but they add another layer of protocol risk.

Oracles are critical because many DeFi systems depend on accurate external data.

Decentralized derivatives introduce collateral, margin, funding, leverage, and liquidation dynamics.

Governance and composability are major strengths of DeFi, but they also create coordination and systemic-risk trade-offs.

This is an intermediate topic. Helpful background knowledge includes blockchain basics, Ethereum basics, wallets, tokens, smart contracts, basic trading terms, and basic lending vocabulary.

Related Campus topics include Ethereum & Layer 1s 201 for smart contracts, gas, and token standards; Market Structure 201 for liquidity, order books, derivatives, and institutions; Scaling & Layer 2s 201 for cheaper execution environments and rollup context; and stablecoins, RWAs, and glossary pages for collateral, settlement, and liquidity context.

DEX: A decentralized exchange that lets users trade through smart contracts rather than a centralized intermediary.

AMM: An automated market maker that prices trades algorithmically using pooled liquidity.

Liquidity pool: A smart contract holding tokens supplied by liquidity providers for traders to swap against.

Liquidity provider: A user or entity that deposits assets into a pool and earns fees while taking pool risk.

Constant product AMM: An AMM model that preserves the invariant x times y equals k.

Concentrated liquidity: A liquidity design that lets LPs provide capital inside selected price ranges.

Intent-based DEX: A DEX model where users express desired outcomes and solvers compete to execute them.

Collateral: Assets pledged to secure a DeFi loan or leveraged position.

Liquidation: The forced closing or sale of collateral when a position becomes unsafe.

Yield aggregator: A protocol that routes funds across strategies to optimize yield.

Oracle: A service that provides external data, such as prices, to smart contracts.

Perpetual futures: Derivative contracts with no expiry that use funding rates to track spot prices.

DAO: A decentralized organization governed through proposals, votes, and smart-contract rules.

Governance token: A token used to vote on protocol decisions or delegate voting power.

Composability: The ability for protocols to plug into and build on top of one another.

DeFi works through smart contracts that coordinate activity such as trading, lending, collateral management, yield strategies, derivatives, and governance on-chain.

An AMM is an automated market maker. It lets traders swap tokens against a liquidity pool, with prices set by an algorithmic formula rather than a traditional order book.

DeFi lending protocols let users supply assets to earn yield or borrow against collateral through smart-contract-based lending markets.

Yield aggregators are protocols that route deposits across strategies and automate parts of yield generation, such as strategy selection and compounding.

DeFi protocols need oracles because many smart contracts depend on external data, especially asset prices used for collateral values, derivatives, and liquidations.

DAO governance uses proposals, voting, delegation, tokens, and treasuries to coordinate protocol decisions and upgrades.

Composability means protocols can connect and build on each other, creating new products while also introducing dependency and systemic-risk trade-offs.

#5059. CFTC Lets Traders Bet on Stocks With Crypto-Style Perpetual Futures

CFTC Lets Traders Bet on Stocks With Crypto-Style Perpetual Futures

The U.S. derivatives regulator has opened the door to perpetual futures on individual stocks, importing one of crypto’s most popular trading instruments into traditional equity markets for the first time.

The Commodity Futures Trading Commission said Tuesday that “equity perpetual futures,” or perpetual contracts tied to individual stocks, can now begin trading on CFTC-registered exchanges.

Perpetual futures, long a staple of crypto markets, are derivatives that track an asset’s price but carry no expiration date, letting traders hold leveraged positions indefinitely as long as they meet margin and funding requirements. Until now, they’ve been largely off-limits to U.S. traders.

Chairman Michael Selig framed the move as the latest step in a campaign to bring perpetual products onshore.

“In my inaugural remarks, I directed the CFTC to use the tools at its disposal to onshore perpetual derivative products so that they can flourish in the U.S., subject to appropriate safeguards,” he said, calling the stock contracts an extension of the agency’s earlier approval of the first true Bitcoin perpetual futures on a regulated U.S. exchange.

That milestone, as Decrypt reported, saw the CFTC clear Bitcoin perpetual futures for Kalshi earlier this year.

Selig returned to a familiar argument: that regulated U.S. venues are safer than the offshore platforms traders have otherwise had to use.

Bringing these products into regulated markets “ensures that American investors and traders can access these products within the protections of the CFTC’s proven regulatory framework, rather than being forced to turn to offshore, and often unregulated, venues,” he said.

It’s a theme he pressed just last week when he used the record Binance hack to argue offshore exchanges need U.S. oversight.

The equity perps were deemed approved as security futures products under the Commodity Futures Modernization Act of 2000, placing them under the joint oversight of the CFTC and the Securities and Exchange Commission.

Selig credited a historic memorandum of understanding the two agencies signed in March for ending the jurisdictional limbo that had, in SEC Chairman Paul Atkins’s words, stranded such products in a regulatory “no man’s land.”

The approval arrives amid a rush to bring perps onshore. The CFTC has floated plans to bring crypto exchanges under federal oversight, dismissed a CME lawsuit challenging crypto perpetual futures, and cleared the way for Coinbase, which recently filed to list single-stock perps on names like Apple, Tesla and Nvidia.

For Selig, the goal is explicit: ensuring “the new frontier of finance is built in the United States of America.”

#5054. BitGo launches institutional trading and financial solutions business led by former NYDIG executive

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

Pete Janney will lead the new BitGo Global Markets division, following the firm's acquisition of NYDIG’s institutional and trading business in August.

Digital asset infrastructure firm BitGo Holdings BitGo Holdings, Inc. (BTGO) announced the launch of BitGo Global Markets on Wednesday, its new institutional trading and financial solutions business bringing together global liquidity, execution, financing, derivatives, and settlement services.

The division will be headed up by former NYDIG executive Pete Janney following BitGo's acquisition of NYDIG's institutional and trading business on Aug. 27, according to a statement shared exclusively with The Block.

About 30 employees joined BitGo following the NYDIG acquisition, bringing experience in derivatives, financing, collateral servicing, and structured products. Janney initially came aboard as head of financial infrastructure and brings extensive experience in trading and capital markets.

BitGo has been offering institutional trading through BitGo Prime since 2020, with that business becoming part of Global Markets alongside the expanded derivatives, collateral management, and structured finance solutions from NYDIG.

"BitGo has built a strong foundation across custody, liquidity, trading, financing, and settlement," BitGo CEO Mike Belshe said in the statement. "Global Markets brings these capabilities together with deeper capital markets expertise, allowing us to support more complex institutional strategies and serve a broader range of our clients' financial needs. Pete understands how institutions trade and manage risk, and I'm excited to have him leading Global Markets.”

Under the new unit, BitGo's Global Liquidity Layer will provide access to spot trading and large over-the-counter transactions, drawing liquidity from exchanges, market makers, and other counterparties, the firm said. Clients can also borrow against their crypto holdings to fund trading positions, with BitGo keeping the collateral in qualified custody, monitoring margins, and handling collateral calls or liquidations when needed.

Additionally, BitGo Global Markets will offer options, swaps, and custom structured products for eligible counterparties to hedge exposure, express market views, and manage portfolio risk, the firm said.

Global Markets will also provide managed investment strategies, private funds, and research, while BitGo's Go Network will handle clearing and settlement.

"Clients who own digital assets need sophisticated solutions that go beyond buy and hold," Janney said. "I look forward to accelerating that work and growing the Global Markets franchise at BitGo."

BTGO was trading up 3.4% in pre-market trading on Wednesday, according to TradingView, following a 3.7% rise on Tuesday.

#5053. Wall Street killed Bitcoin’s wild rallies: 10x’s Markus Thielen

Markus Thielen sees Bitcoin reaching $90,000 by year-end, questions institutional ETF demand and says Ethereum must do more for holders.

Bitcoin’s days of consecutive double-digit price surges are over as Wall Street traders and profit-taking whales reshape the market, according to 10x Research founder Markus Thielen.

Institutional traders selling call options after rallies have made Bitcoin’s violent price explosions of earlier years less common, Thielen says in the latest episode of Cointelegraph’s Trade Secrets.

Thielen is also the author of Crypto Titans, a history of the industry. Writing the book led him to notice how each cycle brought different people pitching crypto to investors.

“Every cycle had actually different promoters, and different promoters bring in different investors,” he says.

This time, the promoters come from Wall Street, where investors trade on economic data and Federal Reserve decisions. Thielen says he expects Bitcoin will rise quickly on news, then spend time crab walking across the chart before the next big macro event.

Thielen estimates that market makers account for up to half of the $60 billion in net spot Bitcoin ETF inflows he analyzed. Those firms hold ETF shares as a way to hedge their options trades, so the positions do not necessarily represent a bet that Bitcoin’s price will rise.

“So in the end it was not outright buying,” he says.

His estimate draws on a review of Form 13F filings, which disclose certain securities holdings of investment managers.

Cumulative net inflows into US spot Bitcoin ETFs. Source: Coinglass

Thielen says the first wave of funds into the ETFs also included retail investors buying through institutions and corporate vehicles. Some paid substantial premiums to the value of the underlying crypto holdings.

“I would still think the institutions could come. I would still think that some of the sovereign wealth funds could come,” Thielen says.

Thielen still sees Bitcoin reaching $90,000 by year-end. He says a drop into the mid-to-high $70,000s could come first.

Bitcoin has already been trading in that range, falling to about $76,100 on Friday following Binance’s $6.3 billion hack. BTC finished October down 7.3%, according to Coinglass.

Thielen compares the recovery in prices over recent months with early 2023, when Bitcoin rose from around $20,000 to $30,000. He says the doubts surrounding this year’s rebound are familiar as investors scan for the next price drop and entry point.

BTC’s return to $90,000 may rest partly on how much further the Fed raises borrowing rates. The central bank increased its benchmark rate by a quarter of a percentage point in September, to a target range of 3.75%–4%.

It held rates steady last Wednesday in a split vote, with two officials voting for another quarter-point increase. The Fed’s statement said inflation was still elevated, leaving open the possibility of further increases.

Thielen expects another hike by December, followed by a more cautious approach to further increases that could help Bitcoin rally toward year-end.

Thielen expects Bitcoin to outperform Ether, saying too much of the value from Ethereum activity goes to layer-2 networks.

Making those networks cheaper to use has been a deliberate part of Ethereum’s development. But cheaper transactions may muddy the investment pitch for ETH.

Ethereum burns base fees paid by users, removing those coins from circulation. Its supply shrinks when the amount burned exceeds new ETH issued to validators. Lower fees can mean less ETH is destroyed.

ETH supply grows when issuance exceeds the amount burned. Source: ultrasound.money

Thielen, who says he has been bearish on Ether for more than a year, compares Ethereum with a highway.

“Everybody wants the highway to be empty so you can go fast, and the roads need to be super, super clean and fixed and everything, but nobody wants to pay for it,” he says.

He singles out Hyperliquid as an example of how trading activity can fund token purchases. The decentralized perpetuals exchange’s assistance fund converts trading fees into HYPE, which is then burned.

For Ethereum, he wants a stronger connection between the network’s use and the financial benefit to holders.

#5052. Crypto Docs Host GitBook Hit by AI Agent Security Breach, Urges Users to Rotate Keys

Crypto Docs Host GitBook Hit by AI Agent Security Breach, Urges Users to Rotate Keys

GitBook, a documentation platform widely used by cryptocurrency projects, has disclosed a security breach involving AI agents that allowed an attacker to gain access to some of its infrastructure credentials, forcing the company to shut down services and revoke customer access tokens.

In a security disclosure published today, the company described the incident as “a sophisticated, highly targeted attack using AI agents.” The attacker gained unauthorized access at 7 p.m. Central European Time on November 2, according to GitBook, which said it detected the intrusion immediately and moved to contain it.

The reference to AI agents is significant. Unlike traditional chatbots that simply respond to questions, AI agents can carry out sequences of actions, interact with software, and perform tasks with limited human supervision, making them potentially useful to attackers trying to automate parts of a cyberattack. Some are even capable of acting autonomously and even against their guidelines, as OpenAI’s and Anthropic’s experiments have proven.

GitBook has not explained exactly how the attacker used these tools, which AI systems were involved, or how the credentials were obtained. Its disclosure also does not establish that GitBook's own AI products were responsible for the intrusion.

The company took its application and documentation sites requiring authentication offline while securing its infrastructure. It also kept publicly available documentation accessible through cached copies for as long as possible, allowing visitors to continue reading previously saved versions of pages even while the underlying systems were unavailable.

A separate status notice acknowledged that GitBook was experiencing downtime affecting its published documentation sites and editor. By Tuesday afternoon, GitBook said its application, API, and published documentation were accessible again, although some services could remain degraded during recovery.

GitBook is effectively an online instruction manual for software products. Cryptocurrency projects use platforms like it to publish technical explanations, developer instructions, and guides that help users understand everything from connecting software to interacting with decentralized financial applications.

The documents themselves are often public, but the systems that maintain them can connect to private accounts and external services. Those connections depend on credentials that are supposed to remain secret, and GitBook says some of them may have been exposed.

Its investigation identified several potentially affected categories, including integration tokens, Git Sync secrets used to connect documentation with code repositories, and API tokens that allow software to communicate with GitBook automatically.

The incident has already prompted a response from the cryptocurrency industry, where documentation websites often serve as an important source of official instructions.

Decentralized exchange PancakeSwap temporarily disabled its documentation following news of the incident, although it said its own systems had not been compromised.

In a public statement, PancakeSwap said its “docs are not compromised and [the] PancakeSwap app is not affected.” The exchange nevertheless took its documentation offline as a precaution and redirected visitors to its homepage.

PancakeSwap reminded users that its documentation does not require them to perform wallet transactions, warning that “docs never ask you to connect your wallet or approve tokens.”

PancakeSwap subsequently announced that its documentation was back online after being moved from GitBook to infrastructure under its own control, with existing documentation links continuing to work.

GitBook says it blocked the attacker, addressed the vulnerabilities involved, replaced its own infrastructure credentials, and invalidated customer API tokens. But customers using GitBook alongside other services still have work to do.

The company's instructions begin with “rotate any existing GitBook API tokens,” meaning customers should replace their old digital access keys with newly generated ones.

GitBook also instructed customers to replace third-party API tokens and secrets used to connect external tools to its platform, including credentials associated with published integrations. Users of its Model Context Protocol, or MCP, server must authenticate again; MCP is a system that allows AI assistants and other software tools to connect to services such as GitBook.

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

Circle booked $182 million in ARC token presale revenue during the quarter, while income from its USDC reserves fell.

Stablecoin issuer Circle Internet Group Circle Internet Group, Inc. (CRCL) reported $865 million in total revenue and reserve income for the third quarter, up 17% from a year earlier, according to its earnings release on Wednesday.

Net income from continuing operations came in at $226 million, compared with $214.4 million in the same quarter last year. Adjusted EBITDA was $317 million, up from $171.5 million in Q3 2025. Circle previously reported $701 million in revenue and reserve income for the second quarter of this year, naming BlackRock, DTCC, and Visa among founding validators for its Arc blockchain.

Operating expenses rose 16% year over year to $245 million in the third quarter, the firm said, citing higher administrative, IT infrastructure, and marketing costs. Distribution, transaction, and other costs fell 8% to $412 million, mostly due to lower distribution payments, it added.

A footnote to the earnings tables also disclosed that Circle had reduced its workforce by approximately 5%, with the company booking $9.1 million in associated restructuring expenses, mainly for notice period and severance payments, employee benefits, and related costs. Circle said the reduction was designed to "better align our organizational structure with our operating model and strategic priorities," but did not give a headcount for the cuts. Circle had approximately 1,100 employees worldwide at the end of 2025, according to its annual report.

Of the $207 million Circle reported in other revenue, $182 million was recognized in Q3 from its ARC token presale. Other revenue had totaled $29 million in the third quarter of 2025. Circle agreed to sell a total of $242.2 million worth of ARC tokens in the presale at a purchase price of $0.30 per token and a $3 billion fully diluted valuation. Circle attributed the 85% increase in adjusted EBITDA to the presale revenue and lower distribution costs, partly offset by spending on new products.

Circle also maintained its full-year 2026 guidance, including $310 million to $330 million in other revenue and $570 million to $585 million in adjusted operating expenses.

Reserve income fell 8% year over year to $658 million as its reserve return rate declined by 61 basis points, the firm said. Circle's USDC stablecoin averaged $73.3 billion in circulation during Q3, an 8% increase from a year earlier. Onchain USDC transaction volume reached $13.9 trillion for the quarter, up 49% year over year. USDC circulation stood at $73.9 billion at quarter-end, unchanged from a year earlier, while Circle's stablecoin market share fell to 27%.

Circle opened Arc's public mainnet on Sept. 16, with more than 100 institutional and ecosystem builders on the network at launch. It also minted the initial supply of 10 billion ARC tokens, which the firm said was a technical milestone, not a commitment to launch them publicly. It expects Arc to move from proof of authority to proof of stake in 2027.

In September, Circle also agreed to acquire Tazapay, a Singapore-based payments firm with more than $25 billion in annualized payment volume. The firm expects the Tazapay acquisition to close in 2027, subject to regulatory approvals. Circle Ventures had led Tazapay's $36 million Series B extension in March. Binance also bought $100 million worth of Circle shares in September and signed a new five-year USDC agreement.

#5045. Justin Sun Accuses Trump-Backed World Liberty of Using UAE Ties to Escape Federal Court

Justin Sun Accuses Trump-Backed World Liberty of Using UAE Ties to Escape Federal Court

Justin Sun’s legal fight with World Liberty Financial now hinges on a question of nationality.

The Tron founder said Tuesday on X that his lawyers have asked a federal court in California to sanction the Trump family-backed crypto venture over how long it took to disclose that its ownership chain gives it citizenship in the United Arab Emirates.

Sun says he is one of World Liberty’s earliest and largest investors. He agreed in November 2024 to buy $30 million of its WLFI tokens and later raised his total investment to $45 million.

In April, he sued the company, alleging it misled him into investing and then unlawfully froze his tokens. World Liberty has fired back in court, alleging that Sun shorted WLFI and drove down its price.

The current dispute turns on a technical point. Sun chose to sue in federal court, which can hear cases between parties from different places but generally can’t hear a foreign plaintiff’s case against a foreign defendant.

As Sun explained it, a company like World Liberty takes on the citizenship of each of its owners, so a single foreign owner makes the whole company foreign for those purposes.

According to Sun, World Liberty’s initial court disclosure listed only itself, with Florida as its sole citizenship. A judge signaled at an August hearing that some of Sun’s claims should stay in open court rather than go to arbitration.

Less than a week later, Sun says, the company amended the disclosure to list 22 members in its ownership chain, with citizenship in Florida, Delaware, Puerto Rico, and the UAE. World Liberty now argues that the federal court lacks jurisdiction over the case, according to Sun.

Sun argues the late disclosure was a tactic to move the case out of federal court, and his motion seeks to make World Liberty and its lawyers bear the cost. He contrasted the company’s position with its own marketing, posting excerpts from World Liberty’s “gold paper” that describe it as “an American, tax-paying, corporation.”

Once the sanctions motion is resolved, Sun said, he will refile his lawsuit in California state court. “I will keep fighting for the transparency this community deserves,” he wrote.

The feud has played out publicly for months. Sun has criticized how World Liberty treats its investors and alleged that the company threatened to burn his WLFI tokens.

#5044. US midterms: Prediction markets favor Democrats in key crypto races

Kalshi and Polymarket price a 75% chance of a Democratic sweep, days after markets misjudged Brazil’s runoff.

Update (Nov. 3 at 10:32 p.m. UTC): This article has been updated to include comments from Talos.

As Americans across the country head to the polls for the US midterm elections Tuesday, prediction market traders have spent months wagering on the outcome. According to an analysis by The Washington Post, nearly $1.9 billion in election-related contracts had traded on Kalshi and Polymarket as of Monday, marking record trading activity for a midterm election cycle.

Markets on both platforms favor Democrats winning control of Congress, with a Democratic sweep priced at 75% on Kalshi and 75.5% on Polymarket as of 4:30 pm UTC. Kalshi traders gave Democrats a 93% chance of taking the House and a 75% chance of winning the Senate, while Polymarket priced those outcomes at 94.5% and 75.5%, respectively.

The vote comes nine days after prediction markets failed to anticipate the outcome of Brazil’s presidential election, with Flávio Bolsonaro favored at roughly 80% the day before incumbent President Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva won the runoff. Kalshi CEO Tarek Mansour reportedly cautioned that “a 5% probability doesn’t mean it won’t happen,” according to CNBC.

For the crypto industry, a shift in congressional control could reshape the prospects for long-awaited regulatory clarity.

A key race for crypto has centered on the Senate seat in Ohio, where Defend American Jobs, part of the Fairshake super PAC network, allocated 54% of its total expenses. According to the official website, $36.9 million was spent on supporting Senator Jon Husted and $19.7 million opposing the staunch crypto critic, Democrat Sherrod Brown. Fairshake spent $40 million to help unseat Brown in 2024.

However, the flood of spending may be to no avail, with traders expecting a win for Brown. The Kalshi market for the race predicted a 72% chance for a Brown win and 72.5% on Polymarket, both in line with a final New York Times/Siena poll that had him ahead 49% to 44%.

A Brown victory could complicate the crypto industry’s legislative agenda, particularly if Democrats regain control of the Senate. Analysts have identified him as a potential chairman of the Senate Agriculture Committee, which oversees the Commodity Futures Trading Commission, although the leadership position remains uncertain.

Related: From Uptober to Hacktober: Are Bitcoin’s seasonal patterns becoming obsolete?

In Maine, however, a Democratic win predicted by traders could help the crypto industry expand its influence in Washington. Republican Senator Susan Collins was one of four Republicans who voted against advancing the CLARITY Act in September, stalling the bill in the Senate.

Kalshi gives Democrat Troy Jackson a 71% chance of unseating her, while Polymarket puts his chances at 71.5%, broadly in line with New York Times polls conducted in October that had Jackson ahead by two percentage points. Advocacy group Stand with Crypto rates Jackson as strongly supportive of crypto, potentially giving the industry another ally in the Senate should he win.

Although many prediction markets are broadly in line with polling results, traders have made clearer calls in three states where polls show close races.

In Alaska, Kalshi gives Democrat Mary Peltola a 71% chance of defeating Republican Senator Dan Sullivan, even though the final New York Times/Siena poll had her ahead by just two percentage points, 48% to 46%. Both candidates have expressed strong support for crypto, according to Stand With Crypto.

In South Carolina, where the candidates were tied at 48% in the New York Times/Siena poll, Kalshi gives Republican Senator Darline Graham, a crypto supporter, a 71% chance of defeating Democrat Annie Andrews.

Iowa remains a toss-up on both platforms, with Democrat Josh Turek overtaking Republican Representative Ashley Hinson on Polymarket at 52%, after months of Hinson being favored to win.

In other key races, prediction markets are broadly in line with the polls. In Michigan, Kalshi gives Democrat Abdul El-Sayed a 79% chance of defeating Republican Mike Rogers. Kalshi also gives Democrat James Talarico a 71% chance of defeating Republican Ken Paxton in Texas, while the final New York Times/Siena poll had Talarico ahead 50% to 43%. A Paxton loss would be a setback for the Digital Freedom Fund, a super PAC backed by Gemini’s co-founders Cameron and Tyler Winklevoss that spent more than $81,000 supporting his campaign. However, Talarico has also expressed support for the crypto industry.

Related: What’s at stake for crypto as the US votes in the 2026 midterms?

Despite the markets leaning towards a Democrat win in both the House and Senate, researchers from Talos have pointed to thin liquidity behind the contracts. According to Talos’ head of research, Kelly Ye, as of 11:30 am ET, just $300,000 of selling would have pushed Kalshi’s Democrats-win-Senate contract down ten points.

The median trade on the markets came in at around $10, with a few large-ticket trades making up the majority of the liquidity behind the contracts.

“As midterm results come in tonight, prediction markets will reprice quickly, but those prices are only as good as the liquidity behind them,” she said in a statement sent to Cointelegraph.

“Whatever happens tonight, one result won’t tell you whether these markets are accurate. Across many settled contracts, outcomes have broadly tracked prices.”

Markets are also starting to look beyond today’s vote to the political and economic landscape following a potential Democratic sweep of Congress. Polymarket’s conditional markets suggest traders are pricing in a higher risk of recession under Democratic control of both chambers, with the implied probability of a US recession by the end of 2027 at 35% if Democrats sweep and 19% if they do not, as of 4:50 pm UTC.

#5040. CrowdStrike Launches AI Agent 'Kill Switch,' Plans to Acquire Fiddler AI

CrowdStrike Launches AI Agent 'Kill Switch,' Plans to Acquire Fiddler AI

CrowdStrike has introduced an AI agent kill switch designed to stop unauthorized activity inside corporate networks, alongside plans to acquire AI observability startup Fiddler as businesses give increasingly autonomous software access to their systems.

The cybersecurity company announced the launch Tuesday at its Fal.Con Europe 2026 conference in Barcelona, expanding its Falcon Guardian security platform with controls that let administrators intervene when AI agents stray beyond their assigned tasks.

The move tackles a problem companies face as they deploy AI systems capable of executing code, accessing sensitive information, and interacting with business applications. Unlike conventional chatbots, these agents can perform sequences of actions with limited human oversight, potentially causing damage even when nobody has deliberately attacked them.

In practice, an agent authorized to organize corporate files might misinterpret its instructions, follow malicious directions embedded in a document, or execute a command its operator never intended. The same access that makes autonomous agents useful can also make their mistakes expensive.

The new AI Kill Switch gives security administrators a way to interrupt an agent's activity without waiting for it to complete its task. According to CrowdStrike's technical explanation, the currently available mechanism works through AI gateways, which can block an agent's access to the models powering its decisions.

The initial release does not yet provide every type of containment CrowdStrike has described. The company plans to expand the feature to terminate agent processes directly on endpoints and coordinate restrictions across cloud environments and other enterprise systems.

An authorized administrator triggers the kill switch, and the action is recorded in an audit trail. CrowdStrike also describes controls to prevent terminated agents from restarting and restrict their network access, with broader enforcement capabilities still under development.

The company is adding protections at other points where agents operate. Falcon Guardian can inspect agent prompts and apply policies to tools including Claude Code, OpenAI Codex, GitHub Copilot, Microsoft 365 Copilot, Cursor, and OpenCode.

The risk is already well documented. As Decrypt previously reported, Microsoft researchers identified a vulnerability in Anthropic's Claude Code GitHub Action that could have allowed attackers to manipulate the coding agent into exposing sensitive credentials through malicious repository content. Anthropic subsequently patched the flaw but Crowdstrike’s solution would be more extensive.

CrowdStrike also signed an agreement to acquire Fiddler AI, a company specializing in monitoring AI models and explaining their behavior.

The deal values Fiddler at roughly $400 million, according to The Wall Street Journal. CrowdStrike has not publicly disclosed the acquisition price, although it said the transaction would be paid primarily in cash, with some stock subject to vesting conditions.

Fiddler's technology addresses a related problem that a kill switch alone cannot solve: understanding what an AI agent was doing before something went wrong, and whether its actions were appropriate in the first place.

Its software evaluates agents before deployment, traces their decisions and tool calls, and monitors changes in behavior after they begin operating. These records can help security teams investigate unexpected actions or provide evidence to auditors.

CrowdStrike expects to complete the Fiddler acquisition during the fourth quarter of fiscal 2027, which ends January 31, 2027, subject to customary closing conditions. Its expanded endpoint kill-switch capabilities have not yet received a specific release date.

#5037. CFTC clears stock perpetual futures for trading on US exchanges

US regulators are opening the door to stock-based perpetual futures, extending a popular crypto trading product into traditional markets amid ongoing legal disputes.

Perpetual futures contracts tied to individual stocks can begin trading on regulated US exchanges for the first time, according to the Commodity Futures Trading Commission (CFTC), extending a derivatives product popularized in cryptocurrency markets into traditional equities.

CFTC Chairman Michael Selig said in a Tuesday statement that the contracts were deemed approved under CFTC Regulation 40.3 and could be offered on exchanges registered with both the CFTC and Securities and Exchange Commission (SEC) to trade security futures products.

Selig said bringing the products into regulated US markets would allow American traders to access perpetual futures under US regulatory safeguards rather than being “forced to turn to offshore, and often unregulated, venues.”

Perpetual futures allow traders to take positions on an asset’s price without owning the underlying asset or facing an expiration date. Crypto exchange BitMEX helped popularize the instruments, commonly referred to as perps, in 2016.

CFTC announces approval of equity perpetual futures trading. Source: CFTC

The announcement follows a wave of regulatory filings in September, when Coinbase, Kalshi and Kraken parent Payward, through its Bitnomial exchange, sought approval to offer perpetual futures tied to individual US stocks. OG.com joined the push days later, proposing contracts that could trade around the clock on weekdays.

The filings followed the CFTC’s May approval of Bitcoin perpetual futures contracts for Kalshi and separate regulatory relief allowing Coinbase to provide access to certain cryptocurrency perpetual futures listed on an affiliated foreign exchange.

Selig did not identify which exchanges would offer the contracts or provide a timeline for when trading would begin.

Related: CFTC sues Utah to block action against prediction markets

The announcement also comes nearly three months after President Donald Trump said the CFTC was working to bring Hyperliquid, a decentralized crypto exchange best known for its perpetual futures trading, into the US in a “fully compliant and legal fashion.”

Trump made the remarks during an Aug. 19 White House event. At the time, neither the CFTC nor Hyperliquid had announced a formal plan for bringing the platform to US markets.

However, the CFTC’s approach to perpetual futures has faced legal opposition. In June, CME Group’s Chicago Mercantile Exchange sued the regulator over its approval of cryptocurrency perpetual futures, arguing that the contracts should be regulated as swaps rather than futures and that the agency had exceeded its authority. The distinction matters because swaps are subject to different regulatory requirements, potentially including stricter reporting, margin and registration rules, while futures can trade under the existing futures exchange framework.

The CFTC moved to dismiss the lawsuit in September, arguing that CME lacked standing because it had not demonstrated financial harm and could list similar contracts itself.

Meanwhile, the Capital Markets Alliance, a coalition that includes Cboe, Citadel Securities and Healthy Markets, filed a lawsuit Tuesday afternoon seeking to overturn the CFTC’s approval.

The group argues that the contracts are security-based swaps under the SEC’s jurisdiction and that the CFTC violated the Administrative Procedure Act by failing to adequately explain its decision or consider the potential impact on investors. The lawsuit also seeks to prevent the approvals from taking effect while the case proceeds.

#5034. SpaceX blocks tokenized stock listing on OKXICE despite offshore trading boom

SpaceX joined several other issuers objecting to OKXICE tokenized trading, months after crypto platforms offered tokens tied to its shares.

Just months after tokenized versions of SpaceX shares became one of the biggest drivers of onchain stock trading, the aerospace company is pushing back against plans to bring similar trading to US investors.

According to a Nov. 2 public notice from OKXICE, SpaceX has objected to the proposed listing of tokenized versions of its shares on the trading venue, a joint venture between crypto exchange OKX and New York Stock Exchange parent Intercontinental Exchange (ICE). The company now joins Tesla and Amazon among 56 companies objecting to the proposed listings.

Under the Securities and Exchange Commission’s five-year Innovation Exemption introduced in September, platforms such as OKXICE can offer trading in tokenized versions of US stocks without registering as national securities exchanges. But the framework also gives companies a say in the process: They have 30 days to object to the proposed trading of their shares tokenized by third parties, effectively keeping those tokens off the venue.

Tokenized stocks are blockchain-based representations of company shares that allow investors to gain exposure to traditional equities onchain, often around the clock. Depending on the structure, they can be backed by actual shares, although the rights granted to tokenholders may differ from those of traditional shareholders.

OKXICE co-chair and former New York Governor Andrew Cuomo told Cointelegraph on Oct. 20 that the venture expected more objections as companies avoided being the first to test the model.

SpaceX’s objection comes despite tokenized versions of its shares already trading on platforms outside the US, where they have attracted significant trading activity since the company’s June public offering.

Ahead of the SpaceX listing, Backpack Securities, a stock brokerage and tokenization platform, and crypto exchange Kraken, through its xStocks offering, both prepared tokenized versions of the aerospace company’s stock, each designed to be backed by actual shares. Backpack’s SPCX token went on to record $1.08 billion in onchain trading volume in June, while Kraken’s SPCXx token recorded $852 million.

Unlike Backpack and Kraken, OKXICE would open tokenized stock trading to US investors under the SEC’s new regulatory framework, potentially tapping into the world’s largest equity market by capitalization and expanding the investor base for tokenized securities.

Nasdaq warned issuers in September that they “must be cautious about their participation” in the venture, arguing that tokenized stock trading could fragment liquidity and undermine long-term shareholder value.

Despite the growing opposition, Cuomo said during an Oct. 30 press conference that as people see the benefits of tokenization, “you’ll see more and more companies participate.”

#5033. Anthropic's AI Is Getting Cheaper to Use. That's Becoming a Problem for Its Business

Anthropic's AI Is Getting Cheaper to Use. That's Becoming a Problem for Its Business

Anthropic is making Claude cheaper to use. Its computing bill, however, is moving in the other direction.

Anthropic said revenue growth from increased usage was "partially offset by a decrease of approximately $600 million from price reductions introduced as part of new model versions and contractual discounts," according to its amended SEC filing.

Meanwhile, compute and infrastructure expenses surged from $5.56 billion to $9.86 billion, an increase of roughly 77%.

That means Anthropic spent roughly 58 cents on compute and infrastructure for every dollar of revenue, versus 48 cents a quarter earlier. The measure includes computing used for research, training, and internal operations—not just serving paying Claude customers—so it isn't a per-prompt profit margin.

The $600 million figure isn't revenue that vanished from Anthropic's books. It's the amount the company says price reductions and discounts offset revenue growth driven by higher usage.

The cuts are not hypothetical. Claude Opus 5.5, which lowered token prices compared with Opus 5, was recently released, being more capable than any previous model, and sometimes even up to par against Fable. Anthropic also introduced Haiku 5.5 in October, pitching a much cheaper model for high-volume work.

Anthropic says Fable 5.1 reduced the cost of typical workloads by around 25%, while Opus 5.5 brought customers' cost per task down roughly 40% compared with Opus 5. The reductions reflect both lower prices and efficiency gains, rather than straight discounts across every task. Anthropic detailed Fable's lower cache-read pricing in its model announcement.

There is a business case for that strategy: cheaper, more capable AI can encourage customers to use more of it. Anthropic says users have historically expanded consumption after such releases.

But its latest quarter also shows how difficult it is to synchronize demand with supply. Anthropic typically reserves compute capacity 12 to 18 months before it becomes available, leaving much of that spending relatively fixed in the near term. It’s also infamous among AI enthusiasts because its quotas tend to deplete too easily.

The quarter included $5 billion of compute under short-term commitments that Anthropic says cost significantly more than comparable capacity secured through multiyear contracts. That expense helped push the company from a $974 million operating profit in the second quarter to a $567 million operating loss in the third.

The longer-term stakes are bigger. Anthropic's IPO filing, means the company is preparing to go public, and put the company in Wall Street’s bulls eye. Its updated disclosures put aggregate lease and purchasing commitments at roughly $895.4 billion, substantially all related to compute and infrastructure.

Reuters has also reported that the filing raised questions about whether infrastructure partners can secure the financing needed to deliver all that capacity. As of now, Google and Nvidia seem to be the most benefited from Anthropic’s growth as its core business depends on their infrastructure.

For now, demand for Claude is still growing rapidly. The harder question is whether Anthropic can keep making its models cheaper faster than the cost of building and running them comes down. Right now, OpenAI, which is also preparing its own IPO offers a competitive model, with many usage resets, and a more generous usage quota.

That’s the main issue keeping Anthropic from rising prices.
